\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\!\!\big).

By definition of
P_{i}
and
because
H
preserves monos, we thus have

P_{i}=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\bar{q}_{i}\cdot\xi)=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\kappa_{Q_{i}}\cdot\xi)
, and
hence we obtain
\xi\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}}
as in (
4.2
) by
the universal property of the coequalizer
\kappa_{P_{i}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\big(\prod_{j\leq i}K_{j}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{P_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\ }
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\kappa_{Q_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\bar{q}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \xi\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hm}

In fact,
\kappa_{P_{i}}
is the regular-epi part of the factorization of
H\bar{q}_{i}\cdot\xi
, and so
Hm\cdot\xi/Q_{i}
is the mono part, and thus
\xi/Q_{i}

is also a monomorphism.

Corollary 4.10
.

If
P_{i}=Q_{i}
for some
i
, then
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}}
carries a unique coalgebra
structure making
\kappa_{P_{i}}
a coalgebra morphism.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Set}
, this means that all states of
X
that are
merged by the algorithm are actually behaviourally equivalent. We
still need to prove the converse, namely that all behaviourally equivalent
states are indeed identified in
X/Q_{i}
:

Theorem 4.10

(Correctness)
.

If
P_{i}=Q_{i}
for some
i
, then
\xi\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}}\colon X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}}\to H(X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}})
is a simple coalgebra.

.

Let

represent a quotient.

(1)

We first prove that for all

,

\text{if $\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}h$ is finer than $Q_{i}$, then $\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}h$ is finer than $P_{i}$}.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

(4)
.

(2)

Next we prove by induction on

that

is finer than
both
P_{i}
and

, for all

. For

, the claim for

is trivial, and the one for

follows
by (
4.3
). In the induction step, we have by the
inductive hypothesis that
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(h)
is finer than
P_{i}
, thus by

4.6
also finer than
Q_{i+1}
and consequently
by (
4.3
) finer than
P_{i+1}
.

(3)

is the regular epi part of

, i.e. we have

Remark 4.12
.

Most classical partition refinement algorithms are parametrized by an initial
partition
\kappa_{\mathcal{I}}\colon X\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{{\ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}{{
{
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\ignorespaces\lxSVG@buttcap\ignorespaces{}
{}
{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.72014pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.96007pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}
. We start with the trivial partition
!\colon X\to 1

because a non-trivial initial partition might split equivalent behaviours and
then would invalidate
Theorem 3
. To accommodate an initial partition

X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}
coalgebraically, replace
(X,\xi)
with the
coalgebra
\langle\xi,\kappa_{\mathcal{I}}\rangle
for the functor

H(-)\times X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}
– indeed, already
P_{0}
will then be finer
than
\mathcal{I}
.

We look in more detail at two corner cases of the algorithm
where the
\mathsf{select}
routine retains all available information,
respectively none.

Remark 4.13
.

If
\mathsf{select}(\hbox to124.86pt{\vbox to14.84pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 62.43047pt\lower-7.42012pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-62.43047pt}{-7.42012pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})=\mathsf{id}_{Y}
(cf.
4.4
(2)
),
then
2
becomes König and Küppers’ final chain
algorithm
[
KK14
]
, as we will now explain.

(1)

Recall that
H
induces the
final chain
:

1\xleftarrow{!}H1\xleftarrow{H!}H^{2}1\xleftarrow{H^{2}!}\cdots\xleftarrow{H^{i-1}!}H^{i}1\xleftarrow{H^{i}!}H^{i+1}1\xleftarrow{H^{i+1}!}\cdots

(The chain is transfinite but we consider only the first
\omega

stages.) Every coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to HX
then induces a

canonical cone

\xi^{(i)}\colon X\to H^{i}1
on the final
chain, defined inductively by

\xi^{(0)}=\mathbin{!}\colon X\to H^{0}1=1\quad\text{and}\quad\xi^{(i+1)}=(X\xrightarrow{\xi}HX\xrightarrow{H\xi^{(i)}}HH^{i}1=H^{i+1}1).

The objects
H^{n}1
may be thought of as domains of
n
-step
behaviour for
H
-coalgebras. If
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Set}
and
X
is finite, then
states
x
and
y
are behaviourally equivalent iff

\xi^{(i)}(x)=\xi^{(i)}(y)
for all
i<\omega

[
Wor05
]
. In fact, Worrell showed this for
unrestricted
X
and for
finitary
set functors
H
,
i.e. set functors preserving filtered colimits; equivalently,
H

is finitary if for every
x\in HX
there exists a finite subset

m\colon Y\hookrightarrow X
and
y\in HY
such that
x=Hm(y)
. Note
that for a
finite
coalgebra for an arbitrary set functor
H
,
behavioural equivalence remains the same when we pass to the

finitary part
of
H
, i.e. the functor given by

H_{f}X=\bigcup\{Hm[Y]\mid\text{$m\colon Y\hookrightarrow X$ and $Y$
finite}\}.

To see this note that if two states in a finite
coalgebra can be identified by a coalgebra morphism into some
H
-coalgebra,
then they can be identified by a coalgebra morphism into a finite

H
-coalgebra. This is just by image factorization of coalgebras
(see
Section 2.2
).

(2)

The inclusions
P_{i}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{{\ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{{
{{{\ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\ignorespaces\lxSVG@buttcap\ignorespaces{}
{}
{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.82 0 L 16.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.19998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L -2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.96007pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}Q_{i+1}
in
4.6

reflect that only some and not necessarily all of the information
present in the relation
P_{i}
(resp. the quotient

X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P_{i}}
) is used for further refinement. If indeed
everything is used, then
Q_{i+1}=P_{i}
, and our algorithm simply computes the
kernels of the morphisms
\xi^{(i)}\colon X\to H^{i}1
forming the
canonical cone:

Proposition 4.14
.

If
\mathsf{select}(\hbox to105.32pt{\vbox to14.84pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 52.65688pt\lower-7.42012pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-52.65688pt}{-7.42012pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\!\!)=\mathsf{id}_{Y}
, then for all
i\in\mathds{N}
,
Q_{i}=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\xi^{(i)}
.

Proof 4.15
.

With
\mathsf{select}(y,z)=\mathsf{id}_{Y}
, we have

q_{i+1}=\kappa_{P_{i}}\colon X\to X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P_{i}}
, and so

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}q_{i+1}=P_{i}
for all
i\in\mathds{N}
. Thus,
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}q_{i+1}
is
finer than
Q_{i}
by
4.6
. It follows that

Q_{i+1}=Q_{i}\cap\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}q_{i+1}=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}q_{i+1}=P_{i}
.

In order to prove that

Theorem 4.17
.

If
\mathsf{select}
is progressing, then
2
terminates and
computes the simple quotient of the input coalgebra
(X,\xi)
,
provided that
(X,\xi)
has only finitely many quotients.

E.g. for
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Set}
, every finite coalgebra has only finitely many quotients.

}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.72014pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.96007pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}}\!\big)
factorizes through
f_{i}
.

To see this, first note that
\mathsf{select}
is discarding at

X/P_{i},X/Q_{i}
iff
q_{i+1}
factorizes through
\kappa_{Q_{i}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{P_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{Q_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}

We thus have the desired equivalence:
q_{i+1}
factorizes through

\kappa_{Q_{i}}
iff (by
Section 2.1
(4)
)
Q_{i}
is
finer than
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}q_{i+1}
iff

Q_{i}=Q_{i}\cap\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}q_{i+1}=Q_{i+1}
.

(2)

We proceed to prove the claim.
4.6
shows that
we obtain a chain of successively finer quotients

X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}}
. Since
X
has only finitely many quotients,
there must be an
i
such that
Q_{i}=Q_{i+1}
, and this implies,
using point (1), that
\mathsf{select}
is discarding at
X/P_{i},X/Q_{i}
.
Since the
\mathsf{select}
routine is progressing, we obtain

P_{i}=Q_{i}
as desired.

5.
Incremental Partition Refinement

In the most generic version of the partition refinement algorithm
(Algorithm
2
), the partitions are recomputed from scratch in every
step: In Step
(3)
of the algorithm,
P_{i+1}=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\langle\bar{q}_{i},q_{i+1}\rangle\cdot\xi)
is computed from the information
\bar{q}_{i}
accumulated
so far and the new information
q_{i+1}
, but in general one cannot exploit that
the kernel of
\bar{q}_{i}
has already been computed. We now present a refinement
of the algorithm in which the partitions are computed incrementally, i.e.
P_{i+1}
is computed from
P_{i}
and
q_{i+1}
. This requires the type functor

H
to be
zippable
(
5.1
) and the
\mathsf{select}
routine to

respect compound blocks
(
5.16
).

Note that in Step
(2)
,
2
computes a kernel

Q_{i+1}=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\bar{q}_{i+1}=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\langle\bar{q}_{i},q_{i+1}\rangle
as the
intersection of
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(\bar{q}_{i})
and
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(q_{i})
(cf. (
2.2
)).
Hence, the partition
X/\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\bar{q}_{i+1}
for such a kernel can be computed in
two steps:

(1)

Compute
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\bar{q}_{i}}
.

(2)

Refine every block in
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\bar{q}_{i}}
with respect to

q_{i+1}\colon X\to K_{i+1}
.

2
can thus be implemented to keep track of the partition

X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}}
and then refine this partition by
q_{i+1}
in
each iteration.

However, the same trick cannot be applied immediately to the
computation of
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P_{i}}
, because of the functor
H

inside the computation of the kernel:

P_{i+1}=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\langle\bar{q}_{i},q_{i+1}\rangle\cdot\xi)
.
In
5.22
, we will provide sufficient conditions for
H
,

a\colon D\to A
,
b\colon D\to B
to satisfy

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}H\langle a,b\rangle=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\langle Ha,Hb\rangle.

As soon as this holds for
a=\bar{q}_{i},b=q_{i+1}
, we can optimize the
algorithm by changing Step
(3)
to

P_{i+1}^{\prime}:=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\langle H\bar{q}_{i}\cdot\xi,Hq_{i+1}\cdot\xi\rangle\quad(=P_{i}\cap\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(Hq_{i+1}\cdot\xi)).

(5.1)

The conditions on
a
and
b
will be ensured by a condition on
\mathsf{select}
, and
the condition on the functor
H
is as follows:

Definition 5.1
.

A functor
H\colon C\to\mathcal{D}
is
zippable

if the following morphisms are monomorphisms for every objects
A

and
B
:

\mathsf{unzip}_{H,A,B}\colon H(A+B)\xrightarrow{\langle H(A+!),H(!+B)\rangle}H(A+1)\times H(1+B)

Intuitively, if
H
is a functor on
\mathsf{Set}
, we may think of
elements
t
of
H(A+B)
as shallow terms with variables from

A+B
. Then zippability means that each
t
is uniquely determined by
the two terms obtained by replacing
A
- and
B
-variables,
respectively, by some placeholder
\mathbin{\_}
, viz. the element of
1
, as
illustrated in the examples in
Figure 2
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}\,a_{2}\,b_{1}\,a_{3}\,b_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}(a_{1}a_{2}\mathbin{\_}a_{3}\mathbin{\_},\\
\phantom{(}\,\mathbin{\_}\mathbin{\_}\,b_{1}\!\mathbin{\_}b_{2})\end{array}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{unzip}}

(a)

(-)^{*}
is zippable

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{a_{1},a_{2},b_{1}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}l}(\{a_{1},a_{2},&\mathbin{\_}\},\\
\{\mathbin{\_},&b_{1}\})\end{array}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{unzip}}

(b)

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
is zippable

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\big\{\{a_{1},b_{1}\},\{a_{2},b_{2}\}\big\}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\big\{\{a_{1},b_{2}\},\{a_{2},b_{1}\}\big\}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}(\big\{\{a_{1},\mathbin{\_}\},\{a_{2},\mathbin{\_}\}\big\},\\
\phantom{(}\big\{\{\mathbin{\_},b_{1}\},\{\mathbin{\_},b_{2}\}\big\})\\
\end{array}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{unzip}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{unzip}}

(c)

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
is not zippable

Figure 2
.
Zippability of
\mathsf{Set}
-Functors for sets

A=\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\}
,
B=\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
H
be zippable and
f\colon A\to C
,
g\colon B\to D
. Then the following
is a mono:

H(A+B)\xrightarrow{\langle H(A+g),H(f+B)\rangle}H(A+D)\times H(C+B)

Proof 5.3
.

By finality of
1
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(A+B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(A+D)\times H(C+B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(A+1)\times H(1+B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle H(A+g),H(f+B)\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{unzip}_{H,A,B}=\langle H(A+!),H(!+B)\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(A+!)\times H(!+B)}

commutes. Since the diagonal arrow is monic, so is

\langle H(A+g),H(f+B)\rangle
.

Assumption 5
.

For the remainder of

Section 5
, we assume that
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Set}
.

However, most proofs are category-theoretic to clarify where working
in
\mathsf{Set}
is really needed and where the arguments are more general.

Example 5.4
.

(1)

Constant functors
X\mapsto A
are zippable:
\mathsf{unzip}

is the diagonal
A\to A\times A
.

(2)

The identity functor is zippable since

\langle A+!,!+B\rangle\colon A+B\to(A+1)\times(1+B)

is monic in
\mathsf{Set}
.

(3)

From Lemma
5.5
it follows that every
polynomial endofunctor is zippable. Indeed, a polynomial functor
is precisely one that is build from constant and the identity
functors using (finite) products and coproducts (cf.
Section 2.2
(6)
).

Lemma 5.5
.

Zippable endofunctors are closed under (possibly infinite) products, coproducts and
subfunctors.

(Recall that products and coproducts of functors are formed
pointwise, e.g.
(F+G)(X)=FX+GX
.)

Proof 5.6
.

For the closure under products and coproducts, we only provide the proof for the
binary case; the proof for arbitrary products and coproducts is
completely analogous. Let
F,G
be endofunctors.

(1)

Suppose that both
F
and
G
are zippable. That
F\times G
is zippable follows from monos being closed under products:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A+B)\times G(A+B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A+1)\times F(1+B)\times G(A+1)\times G(1+B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(F(A+1)\times G(A+1)\big)\times\big(F(1+B)\times G(1+B)\big).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{unzip}_{F,A,B}\times\mathsf{unzip}_{G,A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{unzip}_{F\times G,A,B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(2)

Suppose again that
F
and
G
are zippable. To see that
F+G
is zippable consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A+B)+G(A+B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(F(A+1)\times F(1+B)\big)+\big(G(A+1)\times G(1+B)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(F(A+1)+G(A+1)\big)\times\big(F(1+B)+G(1+B)\big).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{unzip}_{F,A,B}+\mathsf{unzip}_{G,A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{unzip}_{F\times G,A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle(\pi_{1}+\pi_{1}),(\pi_{2}+\pi_{2})\rangle}

The horizontal morphism is monic since monos are closed under
coproducts in
\mathsf{Set}
.
The vertical morphism is monic since for any sets

A_{i}
and
B_{i}
,
i=1,2
, the following morphism clearly is a
monomorphism:

(A_{1}\times B_{1})+(A_{2}\times B_{2})\xrightarrow{\langle(\pi_{1}+\pi_{1}),(\pi_{2}+\pi_{2})\rangle}(A_{1}+A_{2})\times(B_{1}+B_{2}).

(3)

Suppose now that
F
is a subfunctor of
G
via
s\colon F\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{{\ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{{
{{{\ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\ignorespaces\lxSVG@buttcap\ignorespaces{}
{}
{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.82 0 L 16.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.19998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L -2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.96007pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}G
, where
G
is zippable. Then the following diagram
shows that
F
is zippable, too:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A+B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A+1)\times F(1+B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(A+B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(A+1)\times G(1+B).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{unzip}_{F,A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{A+1}\times s_{1+B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{unzip}_{G,A,B}}

Indeed, since the composition of the lower and left-hand morphisms
is monomorphic, so is the upper morphism.

Lemma 5.7
.

If
H
has a componentwise monic natural transformation

H(X+Y)\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{{\ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{{
{{{\ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\ignorespaces\lxSVG@buttcap\ignorespaces{}
{}
{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.82 0 L 16.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.19998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L -2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.96007pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}HX\times HY
, then
H
is zippable.

Proof 5.8
.

Let
\alpha_{X,Y}\colon H(X+Y)\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{{\ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{{
{{{\ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\ignorespaces\lxSVG@buttcap\ignorespaces{}
{}
{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.82 0 L 16.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.19998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L -2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.96007pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}HX\times HY
be monic and
natural in
X
and
Y
. Then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(A+B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(A+1)\times H(1+B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HA\times HB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(HA\times H1)\times(H1\times HB)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{unzip}=\langle H(A+!),H(!+B)\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A,1}\times\alpha_{1,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle HA\times H!,H!\times HB\rangle}

commutes by naturality of
\alpha
. The
bottom morphism is monic because it has a left inverse,

\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}
. Therefore,
\mathsf{unzip}
is monic as well.

Example 5.9
.

(1)

For every commutative monoid, the monoid-valued functor

M^{(-)}
(see
Section 2.2
(2)
) admits a natural
isomorphism
M^{(X+Y)}\cong M^{(X)}\times M^{(Y)}
, and hence is
zippable by Lemma
5.7
.

(2)

As special cases of monoid-valued functors we obtain
that the finite powerset functor
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
and the bag functor
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}

are zippable.

(3)

By
5.7
, the full powerset functor
\mathcal{P}
is zippable.

(4)

The distribution functor
\mathcal{D}
(see Example
2.2
) is
a subfunctor of the monoid-valued functor
\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}^{(-)}
for the
additive monoid
\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
of real numbers, and hence is zippable by
Item
(1)
and Lemma
5.5
.

(5)

The previous examples together with the closure properties in
Lemma
5.5
show that a number of functors of interest
are zippable, e.g.
2\times(-)^{A}
,

2\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(-)^{A}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times(-))
,
2\times\big((-)+1\big)^{A}
,
and variants where
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
is replaced by
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}
,
M^{(-)}
, or

\mathcal{D}
.

Remark 5.10
.

Out of the above results, only zippability of the identity and
coproducts of zippable functors make use of properties of
\mathsf{Set}

(
5
). Indeed, zippable functors on a
category
\mathcal{C}
are closed under coproducts as soon as monomorphisms
are closed under coproducts in
\mathcal{C}
, which is satisfied in most
categories of interest. Zippability of the identity holds whenever

\mathcal{C}
is extensive, i.e. it has well-behaved set-like coproducts (see
e.g.
[
CLW93
]
or
Section 8.2
later). Examples of
extensive categories are the categories of sets, posets and graphs
as well as any presheaf category. We will take a closer look at
extensive categories when we discuss multisorted coalgebras
(
Section 8
).

Example 5.11
.

The monotone neighbourhood functor, which maps a set
X
to the set

\mathcal{M}(X)=\{N\subseteq\mathcal{P}X\mid A\in N\wedge B\supseteq A\implies B\in N\},

of monotone neighbourhood systems over
X
, is not zippable, that
is, there are distinct monotone neighbourhood systems that are
identified by
\mathsf{unzip}
. Indeed, denoting the upwards closure
of a set system by
(-)\mathord{\uparrow}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{unzip}\left(\left\{\{a_{1},b_{1}\},\{a_{2},b_{2}\}\right\}\!\mathord{\uparrow}\right)

\displaystyle=\big(\big\{\{a_{1},\mathbin{\_}\},\{a_{2},\mathbin{\_}\}\big\}\mathord{\uparrow},\big\{\{\mathbin{\_},b_{1}\},\{\mathbin{\_},b_{2}\}\big\}\mathord{\uparrow}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{unzip}\left(\left\{\{a_{1},b_{2}\},\{a_{2},b_{1}\}\right\}\!\mathord{\uparrow}\right).

Example 5.12
.

The functor
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
fails to be zippable, as shown in

Figure 2
. First, this shows that zippable functors are
not closed under quotients, since
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
is a quotient of the
polynomial, hence zippable, functor
HH
where

HX=\coprod_{n<\omega}X^{n}
. Secondly, this shows that zippable
functors are not closed under composition.

The following example shows that the optimized algorithm,
i.e.
2
run with (
5.1
) in lieu of
Step
(3)
, is not correct for the non-zippable functor

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
, even though the
\mathsf{select}
routine used here (see

4.4
(1)
) behaves sufficiently
well (specified later in
5.16
and
cf.
5.26
).

Example 5.13
.

Consider the following coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to HX
for
HX=2\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{6}}

\pi_{1}(\xi(x))=1

(3)
of
2
by equation (

),
i.e. we compute

P_{i+1}=P_{i+1}^{\prime}\overset{\eqref{kernelOptimization}}{=}\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\langle H\bar{q}_{i}\cdot\xi,Hq_{i+1}\cdot\xi\rangle=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}P_{i}^{\prime}\cap\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(Hq_{i+1}\cdot\xi).

and

are identified by all

and

(

For the subblock

because:

\displaystyle H\chi_{\{a_{3},b_{5}\}}^{\{a_{3},b_{5},a_{5},b_{3}\}}\cdot\xi(a_{1})

\displaystyle=H\chi_{\{a_{3},b_{5}\}}^{\{a_{3},b_{5},a_{5},b_{3}\}}\big\{\{a_{2},a_{3}\},\{a_{4},a_{5}\}\big\}

\displaystyle=\phantom{H\chi_{\{a_{3},b_{5}\}}^{\{a_{3},b_{5},a_{5},b_{3}\}}}\big\{\{0,2\},\{0,1\}\big\}

\displaystyle=\phantom{H\chi_{\{a_{3},b_{5}\}}^{\{a_{3},b_{5},a_{5},b_{3}\}}}\big\{\{0,1\},\{0,2\}\big\}

\displaystyle=H\chi_{\{a_{3},b_{5}\}}^{\{a_{3},b_{5},a_{5},b_{3}\}}\big\{\{b_{2},b_{3}\},\{b_{4},b_{5}\}\big\}=H\chi_{\{a_{3},b_{5}\}}^{\{a_{3},b_{5},a_{5},b_{3}\}}\cdot\xi(b_{1})

At this point the algorithm terminates because
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{2}}=X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P_{2}}
, while incorrectly not distinguishing
a_{1}
and

b_{1}
.

Note that this result remains the same if we chose the subblock

\{a_{5},b_{3}\}
in the third iteration or if we chose

\{a_{3},b_{5}\}
in the second iteration and
\{a_{1},b_{1}\}
in the third one.

Observe that, in general,

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}H\langle a,b\rangle
differs from
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\langle Ha,Hb\rangle
even if
H
is
zippable:

Example 5.14
.

For
H=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
and product projections

\pi_{1}\colon A\times B\to A
and
\pi_{2}\colon A\times B\to B
,

\langle\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{1},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{2}\rangle
in general fails to be injective
although
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathsf{id}_{A\times B}=\mathsf{id}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times B)}
. Thus

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle\cong\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times B)\not\cong\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\langle\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{1},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{2}\rangle.

Hence, in addition to zippability of
H
, we will need to enforce constraints on the

\mathsf{select}
routine to achieve the desired
optimization (
5.1
).

The next example illustrates this issue, and a related one: One might
be tempted to implement splitting by a subblock
S
by using the usual
characteristic function
q_{i}=\chi_{S}\colon X\to K_{i}
.
While this approach is sufficient for systems with real-valued
weights
[
VF10
]
, it may in general let

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\langle\bar{q}_{i},q_{i+1}\rangle\cdot\xi)
and

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\langle H\bar{q}_{i}\cdot\xi,Hq_{i+1}\cdot\xi\rangle
differ even if

H
is zippable, thus rendering the algorithm incorrect:

\blacktriangle

for

\blacktriangle

for

Example 5.15
.

Consider the coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to HX
for the zippable functor

H=\{\blacktriangle,\mdblksquare,\smblkcircle\}\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(-)

illustrated in
Figure 3
(essentially a Kripke
model). The initial partition
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P_{0}}
splits the set
of all states by shape and by
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}!
, i.e. states with successors
are distinguished from the ones without
successors (
3(a)
). Now, suppose that

\mathsf{select}
returns
k_{1}:=\mathsf{id}_{X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P_{0}}}
, i.e. retains all information (cf.
4.13
), so that

Q_{1}=P_{0}
and
P_{1}
puts
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
into different blocks
(
3(b)
). Since
q_{0}=\mathord{!}
, we have
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\bar{q}_{1}=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}q_{1}
and thus simplify notation by directly defining

\bar{q}_{1}:=\kappa_{P_{0}}
. We now analyse the next partition that
arises when we split w.r.t. the subblock
S=\{c_{1}\}
but not
w.r.t. the rest
C\setminus S
of the compound block

C=\{c_{1},c_{2}\}
; in other words, we take

k_{2}:=\chi_{\{\{c_{1}\}\}}\colon X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P_{1}}\to 2
, making

q_{2}=\chi_{\{c_{1}\}}\colon X\to 2
. Then,

H\langle\bar{q}_{1},q_{2}\rangle\cdot\xi
splits
t_{1}
from
t_{2}
, because

t_{1}
has a successor
c_{2}
with
\bar{q}_{1}(c_{2})=\{c_{1},c_{2}\}
and

q_{2}(c_{2})=0
whereas
t_{2}
has no such successor. However,

t_{1},t_{2}
fail to be split by
\langle H\bar{q}_{1},Hq_{2}\rangle\cdot\xi

because their successors do not differ when we look at successor
blocks in
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{1}}
and
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\chi_{S}}

separately: both have
\{c_{1},c_{2}\}
and
\{c_{3}\}
as successor
blocks in
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{1}}
and
\{c_{1}\}
and

X\setminus\{c_{1}\}
as successors in

X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\chi_{S}}
(cf.
Figure 4
). Formally:

\displaystyle H\bar{q}_{1}\cdot\xi(t_{1})

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{id}\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\kappa_{P_{0}})\cdot\xi(t_{1})=(\blacktriangle,\big\{\{c_{1},c_{2}\},\{c_{3}\}\big\})=H\bar{q}_{1}\cdot\xi(t_{2}),

\displaystyle Hq_{2}\cdot\xi(t_{1})

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{id}\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\chi_{\{c_{1}\}})\cdot\xi(t_{1})=(\blacktriangle,\big\{0,1\big\})=Hq_{2}\cdot\xi(t_{2}).

So if we computed
P_{2}
iteratively as in
(
5.1
) for
q_{2}=\chi_{S}
, then
t_{1}
and
t_{2}

would not be split, and we would reach the termination condition

P_{2}=Q_{2}
before all behaviourally inequivalent states have been
separated.

Already Paige and Tarjan
[
PT87
, Step 6 of the
Algorithm]
note that one additionally needs to
split by
C\setminus S=\{c_{3}\}
, which is accomplished by
splitting by
q_{i}=\chi_{S}^{C}
(see

4.4
(1)
). This is
formally captured by the condition we introduce next and explain
further in
5.17
.

Definition 5.16
.

A
\mathsf{select}
routine
respects compound blocks
if whenever

k=\mathsf{select}(X\overset{y}{\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

{

{}
{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}Y\overset{z}{\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}Z)

then the union
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}z\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}k
is a kernel.

Since in
\mathsf{Set}
, reflexive and symmetric relations are
closed under unions, the definition boils down to
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}z\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}k

being transitive. In
\mathsf{Set}
, we have an intuitive characterization:

Lemma 5.17
.

For maps
a\colon Y\to A
,
b\colon Y\to B
, the following are
equivalent:

(1)

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b\rightrightarrows Y
is a kernel (i.e. an
equivalence relation).

(2)

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b\rightrightarrows Y
is the kernel of
the pushout of
a
and
b
.

(3)

For all
x,y,z\in Y
,
a(x)=a(y)
and
b(y)=b(z)
implies

a(x)=a(y)=a(z)
or
b(x)=b(y)=b(z)
.

(4)

For all
x\in Y
,
[x]_{a}\subseteq[x]_{b}
or
[x]_{b}\subseteq[x]_{a}
.

The last item states that when we move from
a
-equivalence classes to

b
-equivalence classes, the classes either merge or split, but do not merge
with other classes and split at the same time. In
5(a)
,
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
is transitive, and thus also a kernel. For
x\in\{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\}
,
we have
[x]_{b}\subseteq[x]_{a}
and for
x\in\{y_{4},y_{5}\}
we have

[x]_{a}\subseteq[x]_{b}
. On the other hand in
5(b)
,
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
is not transitive because
(y_{1},y_{3})\in\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
and
(y_{3},y_{5})\in\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a
, but
(y_{1},y_{5})\not\in\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
, and indeed condition

(4)
of
5.17
fails because

[y_{3}]_{a}\not\subseteq[y_{3}]_{b}
and
[y_{3}]_{b}\not\subseteq[y_{3}]_{a}
(because

Proof 5.18

(Proof of
5.17
)
.

(4)
\Rightarrow
(1) In
\mathsf{Set}
, kernels are equivalence relations. Obviously,
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
is both reflexive and symmetric. For transitivity,
take
(x,y),(y,z)\in\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
. Then
x,z\in[y]_{a}\cup[y]_{b}
. If
[y]_{a}\subseteq[y]_{b}
, then
x,z\in[y]_{b}
and
(x,z)\in\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
; otherwise
(x,z)\in\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a
.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2) In
\mathsf{Set}
, monomorphisms are stable under pushouts, so it is
sufficient to show that
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
is the kernel of the
pushout of the regular epis from the image factorization of
a

and
b
, respectively. In other words, w.l.o.g. we may
assume that
a
and
b
are surjective maps, and we need to check that
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
is the kernel of
p:=p_{A}\cdot a=p_{B}\cdot b
,
where
p_{A}
and
p_{B}
are the two injections of the pushout below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{B}}

Let
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
be the kernel of some
f\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}
. Then,

f
makes the projections of
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a
(resp.
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
) equal and hence
the coequalizer
a
(resp.
b
) induces a unique
f_{A}

(resp.
f_{B}
) such that the triangles in the diagrams below commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!f_{A}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!f_{B}}

Since
f_{B}\cdot b=f=f_{A}\cdot a
,
(f_{A},f_{B})
is a competing
cocone for the above pushout. This induces a cocone morphism
m\colon(P,p_{A},p_{B})\to(Y,f_{A},f_{B})
, and we have

m\cdot p=m\cdot p_{A}\cdot a=f_{A}\cdot a=f.

(5.2)

We are ready to show that
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
is a kernel of
p
. By the
definition of
p
, the projections of
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
are made equal by

p
. For the universal property, let
d_{1}\colon D^{\prime}\to D
,
d_{2}\colon D^{\prime}\to D
such that
p\cdot d_{1}=p\cdot d_{2}
. Then we have

f\cdot d_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\eqref{eq:ypp-y}}}}{{=}}m\cdot p\cdot d_{1}=m\cdot p\cdot d_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\eqref{eq:ypp-y}}}}{{=}}f\cdot d_{2}.

This means that
d_{1},d_{2}\colon D^{\prime}\rightrightarrows D
is a competing cone
w.r.t. the kernel of
f
.
We thus obtain a unique cone morphism
u\colon D^{\prime}\to\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
as
desired.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(3) Take
x,y,z\in Y
with
a(x)=a(y)
and

b(y)=b(z)
. Then
a(x)
and
b(z)
are identified in the
pushout
P
:

p(x)=p_{A}\cdot a(x)=p_{A}\cdot a(y)=p_{B}\cdot b(y)=p_{B}\cdot b(z)=p(z).

This shows that
(x,z)
lies in
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
, hence we have
that
a(x)=a(z)
or
b(x)=b(z)
.

(3)
\Rightarrow
(4) For a given
y\in Y
, there is nothing to show
in the case where
[y]_{a}\subseteq[y]_{b}
. Otherwise if

[y]_{a}\not\subseteq[y]_{b}
, then there is some
x\in[y]_{a}
that does
not lie in
[y]_{b}
, i.e. such that
a(x)=a(y)
and
b(x)\neq b(y)
. Now let
z\in[y]_{b}
, i.e.
b(y)=b(z)
. Then, by assumption,

a(x)=a(y)=a(z)
or
b(x)=b(y)=b(z)
. Since the latter does
not hold, we have
a(y)=a(z)
, i.e.
z\in[y]_{a}
.

.

.

(1)

For
S\in Y
and
[S]_{z}\subseteq Y
,
k:=\chi_{\{S\}}^{[S]_{z}}\colon Y\to 3
respects
compound blocks using
Lemma
5.17
(4)
. Indeed,
one proceeds by case distinction on
B\in Y
:

(a)

If
B=S
, then
[B]_{k}=\{S\}\subseteq[S]_{z}=[B]_{z}
.

(b)

If
B\in[S]_{z}
and
B\neq S
, then
k(B)=1
so that

[B]_{k}=[S]_{z}\setminus\{S\}\subseteq[S]_{z}=[B]_{z}.

(c)

Finally, if
B\in Y\setminus[S]_{z}
, then we have

z(B)\neq z(S)
and therefore

[B]_{z}\subseteq Y\setminus[S]_{z}=[B]_{k}
, where the latter
equation follows from
k(B)=0
.

(2)

The
\mathsf{select}
routine returning the identity
\mathsf{id}_{Y}
respects compound
blocks, because for any morphism
z\colon Y\to Z
,
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\mathsf{id}_{Y}\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}z=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}z
is a kernel.

(3)

The constant
k=\mathbin{!}
respects compound blocks, because for all

B\in Y
:
[B]_{z}\subseteq Y=[B]_{!}
.

For every pair
a\colon Y\to A
,
b\colon Y\to B
of maps,
the kernel of
\langle a,b\rangle\colon Y\to A\times B
is the intersection

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cap\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
. If the union
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
is an
equivalence relation, then every block in the partition

Y/\mathord{\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\langle a,b\rangle}
is either is an equivalence class from
Y/\mathord{\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a}

or from
X/\mathord{\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b}
. That this happens can be visually illustrated as
follows (see
5(a)
): Every equivalence class of

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cap\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
already appears in
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a
or in
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
(or
both), i.e. for all
x\in Y
, we have
[x]_{a}=[x]_{\langle a,b\rangle}
or

[x]_{b}=[x]_{\langle a,b\rangle}
. However for
5(b)
,

\{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\}=[y_{3}]_{b}\neq[y_{3}]_{\langle a,b\rangle}\neq[y_{3}]_{a}=\{y_{3},y_{4},y_{5}\}
.
In the following we prove formally that whenever
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b

is an equivalence relation, then every equivalence class of

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\langle a,b\rangle
comes from one of
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a
(i.e. is in the set
A^{\prime}
)
or of
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
(i.e. is in the set
B^{\prime}
).

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
a\colon Y\to A
,
b\colon Y\to B
such that

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
is a kernel. Then there exist sets
A^{\prime}
,
B^{\prime}
,
and maps
m
,
q
,
f_{A}
, and
f_{B}
such that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}+B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle a,b\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}+\bar{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}+B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{B}^{\prime}+B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}+f_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{B}+B^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

Proof 5.21
.

Define the sets

\displaystyle Y_{A}

\displaystyle=\{x\in Y\mid[x]_{a}\subseteq[x]_{b}\},

\displaystyle Y_{B}

\displaystyle=\{x\in Y\mid[x]_{a}\supsetneqq[x]_{b}\},

\displaystyle A^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{a(x)\mid x\in Y_{A}\},

\displaystyle B^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{b(x)\mid x\in Y_{B}\}.

By
5.17
,
Y=Y_{A}+Y_{B}
. Next define

q=a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}\colon Y\cong Y_{A}+Y_{B}\to A^{\prime}+B^{\prime}
, where
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}

are the obvious restrictions of
a
and
b
, respectively. Now
put
\bar{A}^{\prime}:=A\setminus A^{\prime}
,
\bar{B}^{\prime}:=B\setminus B^{\prime}
, and
define
f_{A}\colon B^{\prime}\to\bar{A}^{\prime}

and
f_{B}\colon A^{\prime}\to\bar{B}^{\prime}
by

f_{A}(b(x))=a(x)\qquad f_{B}(a(x))=b(x).

These functions are well-defined by the definition of
Y_{A}
and

Y_{B}
. Moreover, the
codomain of
f_{A}
restricts to
\bar{A}^{\prime}
, because
a(x)\in A^{\prime}

implies that
[x]_{a}\subseteq[x]_{b}
, contradicting
x\in Y_{B}
.
Analogously, the codomain of
f_{B}
restricts to
\bar{B}^{\prime}
. Let
m

be the unique map such that the right-hand diagram above commutes,
i.e.
m
is induced by the universal property of the product
A\times B
. Since
(\mathsf{id}_{A^{\prime}}+f_{A})\cdot q=a
and
(f_{B}+\mathsf{id}_{B^{\prime}})\cdot q=b
, we see that the left-hand diagram above
commutes.

This factorization is the main ingredient making
H

essentially commute with
\langle-,-\rangle
:

Proposition 5.22
.

Let
a\colon Y\to A
,
b\colon Y\to B

such that
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}a\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}b
is a kernel, and let

H\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
be a zippable functor. Then we have

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\langle Ha,Hb\rangle=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}H\langle a,b\rangle.

(5.3)

Proof 5.23
.

Using the additional data provided by
5.20
, we note that
the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(A\times B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(A^{\prime}+B^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(A^{\prime}+\bar{A}^{\prime})\times H(\bar{B}^{\prime}+B^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HA\times HB}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\langle a,b\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle H\pi_{1},H\pi_{2}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle H(A^{\prime}+f_{A}),H(f_{B}+B^{\prime})\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hm}

By
5.2
, the composition at the bottom is a
mono, because
H
is zippable. Hence,
Hm
is a mono as well.
We conclude

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\langle Ha,Hb\rangle=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\big(\langle H\pi_{1},H\pi_{2}\rangle\cdot H\langle a,b\rangle\big)=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(Hq)=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}H\langle a,b\rangle,

using
Section 2.1
(2)
in the last two equalities.

Remark 5.24
.

Note that
5.22
holds more generally for every
functor
H\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathcal{D}
, where
\mathcal{D}
is a finitely
complete category.

For a
\mathsf{select}
routine respecting compound blocks, we
can now apply
5.22
to prove the equivalence of
(
5.1
) and Step
(3)
of
2
:

Theorem 5.24
.

If
H\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
is zippable and
\mathsf{select}
respects
compound blocks, then the optimization (
5.1
) is
correct.

Proof 5.25
.

Correctness of (
5.1
) means that

P_{i+1}=P_{i+1}^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\langle\bar{q}_{i},q_{i+1}\rangle\cdot\xi)=P_{i}\cap\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(Hq_{i+1}\cdot\xi).

Indeed, suppose that
H
is zippable, and let
k=\mathsf{select}(\kappa_{P_{i}},f_{i})
, where
f_{i}\colon X/P_{i}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{{\ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}{{
{
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\ignorespaces\lxSVG@buttcap\ignorespaces{}
{}
{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.72014pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.96007pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}X/Q_{i}
witnesses that
P_{i}
is finer than

Q_{i}
(see
2
). Then we have

q_{i+1}=k\cdot\kappa_{P_{i}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bar{q}_{i}=m\cdot\kappa_{Q_{i}}=m\cdot f_{i}\cdot\kappa_{P_{i}},

(5.4)

where
m
is obtained by the image factorization of
\bar{q}_{i}
. By

Section 2.1
(2)
we have
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}f_{i}=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(m\cdot f_{i})
. Since
\mathsf{select}
respects compound blocks we know that

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}f_{i}\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}k
is a kernel, thus so is
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(m\cdot f_{i})\cup\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}k
. By
5.22
, we obtain

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\langle H(m\cdot f_{i}),Hk\rangle=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}H\langle m\cdot f_{i},k\rangle,

which, using
Section 2.1
(5)
, implies

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(\langle H(m\cdot f_{i}),Hk\rangle\cdot H\kappa_{P_{i}}\cdot\xi)=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\langle m\cdot f_{i},k\rangle\cdot H\kappa_{P_{i}}\cdot\xi).

(5.5)

Thus we obtain the desired result:

\displaystyle P_{i}\cap\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(Hq_{i+1}\cdot\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\big(\langle H\bar{q}_{i}\cdot\xi,Hq_{i+1}\cdot\xi\rangle\big)

def. of
P_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\big(\langle H(m\cdot f_{i}),Hk\rangle\cdot H\kappa_{P_{i}}\cdot\xi\big)

by (
5.4
)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\langle m\cdot f_{i},k\rangle\cdot H\kappa_{P_{i}}\cdot\xi)

by (
5.5
)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\big(H\langle\bar{q}_{i},q_{i+1}\rangle\cdot\xi\big)

by (
5.4
)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\big(H\bar{q}_{i+1}\cdot\xi\big)

def. of
\bar{q}_{i+1}

\displaystyle=P_{i+1}

\displaystyle\text{def.~of $P_{i+1}$}.

Combining
Theorem 6
and
Theorem 4

we obtain:

Corollary 5.26
.

Suppose that
H
is a zippable endofunctor and that

\mathsf{select}
respects compound blocks and is progressing. Then
2
with
optimization (
5.1
) terminates and computes the
simple quotient of a given finite
H
-coalgebra.

Remark 5.27
.

Note that all results in this section can be formulated and proved
in a Boolean topos
\mathcal{C}
in lieu of
\mathsf{Set}
, e.g. the category of
nominal sets and equivariant maps. In particular, the set-theoretic
statements in
5.17
and

5.20
can be formulated in the internal language of
a Boolean topos, i.e. the ordinary set theory ZF (with bounded
quantifiers only) without the axiom of choice, but with the law of
excluded middle. A detailed definition and discussion of this
language can be found, e.g. in Mac Lane and
Moerdijk
[
MM92
]
.

6.
Refinement Interfaces

In
2
, we left
unspecified how the kernels and partitions can be computed
efficiently. In this section we introduce the notion of a

refinement interface
for the given type functor. This interface
is aimed at efficient computation of the partition
X/P
in

2
, both in the initialization and in the

refinement step
, i.e. in the optimization
(
5.1
) of Step
(3)
in
2
. In

Section 7
we will assume that the type functor
H
comes
equipped with a refinement interface which is used as a parameter for
a generic initialization procedure and an implementation
of (
5.1
) such that partition refinement on a
coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to HX
with
n=|X|
states and
m
edges runs
in
\mathcal{O}((m+n)\cdot\log n)
. In order to phrase and actually achieve
this complexity bound, we need a notion of edges in coalgebras. This
notion will also provide a representation of coalgebras, for purposes
of using them as inputs to the partition refinement algorithm:

Definition 6.1
.

An
encoding
of a functor
H
consists of a set
A
of

labels
and a family of maps

\flat\colon HX\to\mathcal{B}(A\times X)
, one for every set
X
. The

encoding
of an
H
-coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to HX
is given by
the map

\langle H!,\flat\rangle\cdot\xi\colon X\to H1\times\mathcal{B}(A\times X)
and we
say that the coalgebra has
n=|X|
states and

m=\sum_{x\in X}|\flat(\xi(x))|
edges.

The purpose of these functions is to enable a representation
of an input coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to HX
as a labelled graph. In
concrete examples, an encoding expresses how one intuitively
visualizes coalgebras (see e.g.
Figure 1
,

5.13
, and
Figure 3
). The reader
should note here that standard partition refinement for graphs on the
encoding does
not
correctly minimize the input coalgebra, that
is, an encoding does by no means provide a reduction of the
minimization problem from coalgebras to graphs
(
Figure 1
illustrates how Markov chain lumping and
bisimilarity in transition systems lead to different
partitions). Technically speaking, behavioural equivalence in

H
-coalgebras is not the same as behavioural equivalence in

H1\times\mathcal{B}(A\times(-))
-coalgebras. Moreover, it is not even
assumed that the map
\flat:HX\to\mathcal{B}(A\times X)
in an encoding is
natural in
X
.

Example 6.2
.

(1)

For
H=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
, the labels are a singleton set
A=1
and we define

\flat\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X\to\mathcal{B}(1\times X)\cong\mathcal{B}(X)
to be the obvious
inclusion
\flat(t)(x)=1
if
x\in t
and
\flat(t)(x)=0
if
x\not\in t
.

(2)

For
HX=\mathds{R}^{(X)}
, the labels are non-zero real numbers
A=\mathds{R}_{\neq 0}
, and
\flat\colon\mathds{R}^{(X)}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathds{R}_{\neq 0}\times X)
is defined as
\flat(t)(r,x)=1
if
t(x)=r\neq 0
and
\flat(t)(r,x)=0
if
t(x)\neq r
or
t(x)=0
. An example
of this encoding is visualized in
1(b)
.

(3)

For a polynomial functor
H_{\Sigma}
(see

Section 2.2
(6)
), we have an encoding where the
label set
A=\mathds{N}
describes the ordering of the components:

\flat\colon H_{\Sigma}X\to\mathcal{B}(\mathds{N}\times X)
is defined by

\flat(\sigma(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}))=\{(1,x_{1}),\ldots,(n,x_{n})\}
for
an
n
-ary symbol
\sigma\in\Sigma
. Note that the set
H_{\Sigma}1
may
be identified with the signature
\Sigma
so

H!\cdot\xi
assigns to each state
x
of a
H_{\Sigma}
-coalgebra its
output symbol, and the bag
\flat\cdot\xi(x)
contains
the successor states of
x
together with their corresponding input
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Having a coalgebra encoded in terms of labelled edges, the following
computational task is solved in the refinement step: Given a coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to HX
as its encoding
X\to H1\times\mathcal{B}(A\times X)
and partitions

X/P
and
X/Q
such that
P
is stable w.r.t.
Q
(see
Section 4
), how
does splitting a block
C\in X/Q
into
S\subseteq C
and
C\setminus S
affect
the partition
X/P
? The explicit case for
H=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
is visualized in

Figure 6
and is computed as follows:

Example 6.3
.

For the finite powerset functor, fix a coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X
. We say
that there is an edge from
x
to a subset
S\subseteq X
if
x
has a
successor node in
S
.

In the Paige-Tarjan algorithm
[
PT87
]
, when a block

C\in X/Q
is split into smaller blocks
S\subseteq C
and

C\setminus S
, every block of
B\in X/P
is split into (at most)
three blocks as visualized in
Figure 6
:

(1)

States
x\in B
with edges to
S
but not to
C\setminus S
.

(2)

States
x\in B
with edges to both
S
and
C\setminus S
.

(3)

States
x\in B
with edges to
C\setminus S
but not to
S
.

It is one of the key points in the analysis of the overall time
complexity that this split is performed in linear time in the number
of ingoing edges into
S
, regardless of the sizes of
C\setminus S

and
B
. Roughly, this works as follows. All edges from a state

x\in X
to the same block
C\in X/Q
share an integer counter
variable that stores how many such edges exist. Sharing a variable
means that every edge is equipped with a pointer to a memory cell
holding the actual counter. For example, in

Figure 6
,
x_{2}\to y_{2}
,
x_{2}\to y_{4}
, and

x_{2}\to y_{3}
share a variable with the value 3. When splitting
C

into
S
and
C\setminus S
, we count the edges ending in
S
and
then compare the result with the counter variable of each of those
edges, which allows us to implement the above three-way split by
iterating over the source nodes
x\in B
with edges to
S
:

x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
y_{1}
y_{2}
y_{3}
y_{4}
\ldots
\ldots

B
C
S
C\setminus S
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
X/P:
X/Q:
X
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X
\scriptstyle\xi
✂
✂
✂

Figure 6
.
The refinement step for
\xi\colon X\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X
, for
S\subseteq C\in X/Q
.

(1)

If the counters match, then all edges from
x
to
C
go indeed to
S
,
and we move all these
x
to a new block.

(2)

If the values mismatch, then some edges from
x
to
C
go to

S
and some to
C\setminus S
. Hence, we move all these
x
to a
new block and then update the counter variables. To this end, we
count the number
n
of edges from
x
to
S
. We re-use the
existing counter variable for edges
x\to C
and make it the
variable for
x\to C\setminus S
by decrementing it by
n
. For
example, in
Figure 6
the counter for the edge

x_{2}\to y_{2}
is decremented from
3
to
2
, and this counter
variable is shared with
x_{2}\to y_{3}
and
x_{2}\to y_{4}
, so the
count for these edges is correctly decremented as well. We then
allocate a new counter with value
n
and change the pointer of
all edges
x\to S
to this new counter. In the example in

Figure 6
, the pointer of the edge
x_{2}\to y_{2}
now
points to this new counter, which holds the value
1
.

(3)

All the remaining
x\in B
have no edge to
S
, and
nothing needs to be done for them, because the counters for edges
from
x
need not change.

We thus have refined the partition
X/P
such that it becomes
(again) stable w.r.t. the new partition
X/Q
where
C
is split
into
S
and
C\setminus S
.

Note that in
6.3
, the block
B
is
split into smaller blocks in such a way that elements
x,x^{\prime}
end up in
the same block iff
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\chi_{S}^{C}(\xi(x))=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\chi_{S}^{C}(\xi(x))
.
This split is done efficiently by maintaining an integer variable for
counting edges. The present section generalizes this procedure from

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
to a
\mathsf{Set}
-functor
H
that implements a so-called

refinement interface
, in which the integer counter generalizes
to a set
W
“weights” and the comparison of counters
generalizes to an
\mathsf{update}
function:

Definition 6.4
.

A
refinement interface
for a
\mathsf{Set}
-functor
H
equipped with
a functor encoding is formed by a set
W
of
weights
and
functions

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}c@{\quad}l}\mathsf{init}\colon H1\times\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}A\to W,&\mathsf{update}\colon\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}A\times W\to W\times H3\times W\end{array}

such that there exists a family of
weight maps

w\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X\to(HX\to W)

such that for all

S\subseteq C\subseteq X
, the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H1\times\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{init}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle H!,\mathsf{fil}_{X}\cdot\flat\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w(X)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}A\times W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\times H3\times W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{update}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle w(S),H\chi_{S}^{C},w(C\setminus S)\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathsf{fil}_{S}\cdot\flat,w(C)\rangle}

(6.1)

commute, where
\mathsf{fil}_{S}\colon\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times X)\to\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(A)

is the filter function

\mathsf{fil}_{S}(f)(a)=\textstyle\sum_{y\in S}f(a,y)

for
S\subseteq X
(e.g.
\mathsf{fil}_{X}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{1}
).

Remark 6.5
.

(1)

Note that for a coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to HX
,
\mathsf{fil}_{S}\cdot\flat\cdot\xi(x)
is the bag of labels of all edges from
x
to
S\subseteq X
in the encoding of
\xi
.

(2)

Observe that
\mathsf{fil}_{S}
is natural in
A
, i.e. for every
map
h\colon A\to A^{\prime}
the following square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(A^{\prime}\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}A^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fil}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(h\times X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fil}_{S}}

Indeed, we have for all
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times X)

\displaystyle\mathsf{fil}_{S}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(h\times X)(f)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{fil}_{S}\big((a^{\prime},x)\mapsto\sum_{(h\times X)(a,y)=(a^{\prime},x)}f(a,y)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{fil}_{S}\big((a^{\prime},x)\mapsto\sum_{h(a)=a^{\prime}}f(a,x)\big)

\displaystyle=a^{\prime}\mapsto\sum_{y\in S}\sum_{h(a)=a^{\prime}}f(a,y)

\displaystyle=a^{\prime}\mapsto\sum_{h(a)=a^{\prime}}\sum_{y\in S}f(a,y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}h\big(a\mapsto\sum_{y\in S}f(a,y)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}h\cdot\mathsf{fil}_{S}(f).

Informally, for a given coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to HX
, the above
axioms for
\mathsf{init}
and
\mathsf{update}
can be understood as a kind of
contract that their implementation for a given functor needs to
fulfil:
\mathsf{init}
receives in its first argument the information which
states of
X
are (non-)terminating, in its second argument the bag of
labels of all outgoing edges of a state
x\in X
in the graph
representation of
\xi
, and it returns the total weight of those
edges. The operation
\mathsf{update}
receives a pair consisting of the bag
of labels of all edges from some state
x\in X
into the set

S\subseteq X
and the weight of all edges from
x
to

C\subseteq X
, and from only this information (in particular

\mathsf{update}
does not know
S
and
C
explicitly) it computes the
triple consisting of the weight of edges from
x
to
S
, the result
of
H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)
and the weight of edges from
x
to

C\setminus S
(in
6.3
, the number of edges
from
x
to
S
, the value for the three way split, and the number of
edges from
x
to
C\setminus S
). The significance of the set
H3
is that when using a
set
S\subseteq C\subseteq X
as a splitter, we want to split every
block
B
in such a way that it becomes
compatible
with
S
and

C\setminus S
, i.e. we group the elements
x\in B
by the value of

H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)\in H3
. The set
W
depends on the
functor. But in most cases
W=H2
and

w(C)=H\chi_{C}\colon HX\to H2
are sufficient.

In implementations, the refinement interface does not need to
provide
w
explicitly, because the algorithm will compute the values
of
w
incrementally using (
6.1
), and
\flat
need
not be implemented because we assume the input coalgebra to be already

encoded
via
\flat
.

Example 6.6
.

The refinement interface for
the powerset functor
H=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
needs to count the edges into blocks

C
, so that we know in the refinement with
S\subseteq C
whether
there are edges to
C\setminus S
, as in

6.3
. For a natural number
n
, we define
the auxiliary function

(-)>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0\colon\mathds{N}\to 2\qquad\text{by }(n>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0)=\min(n,1)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $n>0$}\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}

For the refinement interface we use
the following weights and labels:

A=1,\quad W=2\times\mathds{N},\quad\text{$w\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X\to(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X\to 2\times\mathds{N})$ with
$w(C)(t)=(|t\setminus C|>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0,|t\cap C|)$.}

For a map
\xi\colon X\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X
, a state
x\in X
, and a block

C\subseteq X
, the weight
w(C)(\xi(x))
is a tuple whose first
component tells us whether there is any edge from
x
to

X\setminus C
and whose second component is the number of edges from

x
to
C
. Since we have a singleton label alphabet
A=1
, every
bag of labels is just a natural number, because

\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}A=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}1\cong\mathds{N}
. The functions

\mathsf{init}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}1\times\mathds{N}\to 2\times\mathds{N}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{update}\colon\mathds{N}\times(2\times\mathds{N})\to(2\times\mathds{N})\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}3\times(2\times\mathds{N})

in the refinement interface for
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
are implemented as follows:

\mathsf{init}(z,n)=(0,n)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{r@{\,}l@{}l}\mathsf{update}(n_{S},(r,n_{C}))&=\big(&(r\vee(n_{C\setminus S}>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0),n_{S}),\\[5.0pt]
&&(r,n_{C\setminus S}>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0,n_{S}>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0),\\[5.0pt]
&&(r\vee(n_{S}>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0),n_{C\setminus S})\big),\end{array}

where
n_{C\setminus S}:=\max(n_{C}-n_{S},0)
,

\vee\colon 2\times 2\to 2
is disjunction, and the middle return
value in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}3
is written as a bit vector of length three. The
axioms in (
6.1
) ensure that

n_{S},n_{C},n_{C\setminus S}
can be understood as the numbers of
edges to
S
,
C
, and
C\setminus S
, respectively.

For the verification of (
6.1
), note that for all
S\subseteq X
,
the map
\mathsf{fil}_{S}\cdot\flat\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X\to\mathds{N}
is given by

\mathsf{fil}_{S}(\flat(t))=|t\cap S|
. Hence, we see that for all
t\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X
we have

\mathsf{init}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}!(t),\mathsf{fil}_{X}(\flat(t)))=(0,\mathsf{fil}_{X}(\flat(t)))=(|t\setminus X|>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0,|t\cap X|)=w(X)(t).

In the verification of the axiom of
\mathsf{update}
, the parameters expand as
follows:

\mathsf{update}(\mathsf{fil}_{S}(\flat(t)),w(C)(t))=\mathsf{update}(|t\cap S|,(|t\setminus C|>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0,|t\cap C|))\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\,}l}n_{S}&=|t\cap S|\\
r&=|t\setminus C|>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0\\
n_{C}&=|t\cap C|\\
\end{array}\right.

So
n_{C\setminus S}=\max(n_{C}-n_{S},0)=\max(|t\cap C|-|t\cap S|,0)=|t\cap(C\setminus S)|
, since
S\subseteq C
. The remaining steps of the
verification are performed component-wise: for
x=\mathsf{update}(\mathsf{fil}_{S}(\flat(t)),w(C)(t))
we have

\displaystyle\qquad\pi_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=\big((|t\setminus C|>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0)\vee(|t\cap(C\setminus S)|>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0),|t\cap S|\big)

\displaystyle=\big(|t\setminus S|>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0,|t\cap S|\big)=w(S)(t),

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=(|t\setminus C|>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0,|t\cap(C\setminus S)|>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0,|t\cap S|>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0)

\displaystyle=(\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in t\\
\chi_{S}^{C}(y)=0\end{subarray}}\!\!1,\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in t\\
\chi_{S}^{C}(y)=1\end{subarray}}\!\!1,\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in t\\
\chi_{S}^{C}(y)=2\end{subarray}}\!\!1)\qquad\qquad\text{as an element of $\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}3$}

\displaystyle=\{\chi_{S}^{C}(y)\mid y\in t\}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\chi_{S}^{C}(t),

\displaystyle\pi_{3}(x)

\displaystyle=\big((|t\setminus C|>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0)\vee(|t\cap S|>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0),|t\cap(C\setminus S)|\big)

\displaystyle=\big(|t\setminus(C\setminus S)|>^{\!\!\smash{?}}0,|t\cap(C\setminus S)|\big)=w(C\!\setminus\!S)(t).

Example 6.7
.

In the following examples, we always
take

W=H2\qquad\text{and}\qquad w(C)=H\chi_{C}\colon HX\to H2.

We also use the helper function

\mathsf{val}:=\langle H(=2),\mathsf{id}_{H3},H(=1)\rangle\colon H3\to H2\times H3\times H2,

where
(=x)\colon 3\to 2
is the equality check for
x\in\{1,2\}
, and in
each case define

\mathsf{update}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}A\times H2\xrightarrow{\mathsf{up}}H3\xrightarrow{\mathsf{val}}H2\times H3\times H2),

for a function
\mathsf{up}\colon\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}A\times H2\to H3
that is defined
individually for every functor.

For the verification of (
6.1
) note that, in
general, for
S\subseteq C\subseteq X
, we have

\mathsf{val}\cdot H\chi_{S}^{C}=\langle H\chi_{S},H\chi_{S}^{C},H\chi_{C\setminus S}\rangle.

Hence, to verify the axiom for

\mathsf{update}=\mathsf{val}\cdot\mathsf{up}
it suffices to verify that

\mathsf{up}\cdot\langle\mathsf{fil}_{S}\cdot\flat,H\chi_{C}\rangle=H\chi_{S}^{C};

(6.2)

in fact, using
w(C)=H\chi_{C}
we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{update}\cdot\langle\mathsf{fil}_{S}\cdot\flat,w(C)\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathsf{val}\cdot\mathsf{up}\cdot\langle\mathsf{fil}_{S}\cdot\flat,H\chi_{C}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathsf{val}\cdot H\chi^{C}_{S}

\displaystyle=\langle H\chi_{S},H\chi^{C}_{S},H\chi_{C\setminus S}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle w(S),H\chi^{C}_{S},w(C\setminus S)\rangle.

(1)

For the monoid-valued functor
H=G^{(-)}\!

over an Abelian group
(G,+,0)
, we take labels
A=G
and define

\flat(f)=\{(f(y),y)\mid y\in X,f(y)\neq 0\}
(which is
finite because
f
is finitely supported). With
W=H2=G\times G
, the
weight
w(C)=H\chi_{C}\colon HX\to G\times G
assigns to
f\in HX=G^{(X)}

the pair of accumulated weights of
X\setminus C
and
C
under

f
:

w(C)(f)=\Big(\sum_{y\in X\setminus C}f(y),\sum_{y\in C}f(y)\Big).

The remaining functions are

\displaystyle\mathsf{init}(h_{1},e)=(0,\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\sum$}e)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{up}(e,(r,c))=(r,c-\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\sum$}e,\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\sum$}e),

where
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\sum$}\colon\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}G\to G
is the obvious summation map
assigning to a bag of elements of
G
their sum in
G
.

Then for all
f\in HX=G^{(X)}
and
S\subseteq C\subseteq X
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{init}(H!(f),\mathsf{fil}_{X}\cdot\flat(f))

\displaystyle=\big(0,{\textstyle\sum}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{1}\cdot\flat(f)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(0,\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in X\end{subarray}}}f(y)\big)=G^{(\chi_{X})}(f)=w(X)(f),

\displaystyle\mathsf{up}(\mathsf{fil}_{S}(\flat(f)),H\chi_{C}(f))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{up}\big(\{f(y)\mid y\in S\},\big(\sum_{\mathclap{y\in X\setminus C}}f(y),\sum_{\mathclap{y\in C}}f(y)\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\sum_{\mathclap{y\in X\setminus C}}f(y),\sum_{\mathclap{y\in C}}f(y)-\sum_{\mathclap{y\in S}}f(y),\sum_{\mathclap{y\in S}}f(y)\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathclap{S\subseteq C}}{=}~~\big(\sum_{\mathclap{y\in X\setminus C}}f(y),\sum_{\mathclap{y\in C\setminus S}}f(y),\sum_{\mathclap{y\in S}}f(y)\big)=H\chi_{S}^{C}(f),

which verifies (
6.2
) and therefore (
6.1
).

(2)

As a special case, we obtain a refinement
interface for the functor
\mathds{R}^{(-)}
, and from this we can derive one
for the distribution functor
\mathcal{D}
, a subfunctor of
\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}^{(-)}
, the following

\mathsf{init}
and
\mathsf{up}
functions:

\mathsf{init}(h_{1},e)=(0,1)\in\mathcal{D}2\subset[0,1]^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{up}(e,(r,c))=(r,c-\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\sum$}e,\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\sum$}e),

if the latter lies in
\mathcal{D}3
, and
\mathsf{up}(e,(r,c))=(0,0,1)
otherwise.

The axiom for
\mathsf{init}
clearly holds since for every

f\in\mathcal{D}X
, we have

\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\sum$}\mathcal{B}\pi_{1}\cdot\flat(f)=\sum_{y\in X}f(y)=1
.

The axiom (
6.2
) for
\mathsf{up}
is proved as in the
previous example; in fact, note that for an
f\in\mathcal{D}X
all
components of the triple

\big(\sum_{y\in X\setminus C}f(y),\sum_{y\in C\setminus S}f(y),\sum_{y\in S}f(y)\big)

are in
[0,1]
and their sum is
\sum_{y\in X}f(y)=1
. Thus,
this triple lies in
\mathcal{D}3
and is equal to
\mathcal{D}\chi^{C}_{S}(f)
.

(3)

Similarly, one obtains a refinement interface for
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}=\mathds{N}^{(-)}
, adjusting the one for
\mathds{Z}^{(-)}\!
; in fact,

\mathsf{init}
remains unchanged and
\mathsf{up}(e,(r,c))=(r,c-\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\sum$}e,\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\sum$}e)
if the middle component is a natural
number and
(0,0,0)
otherwise.

To verify (
6.1
) for the refinement interface for

\mathds{N}^{(-)}
we argue similarly as in point
(2)
above: If

f
lies in
\mathds{N}^{(X)}\subseteq\mathds{Z}^{(X)}
, i.e. has only non-negative components,
then only non-negative components appear in the data returned by
\mathsf{up}
and

\mathsf{update}
, so
\mathsf{up}
and
\mathsf{update}
restrict from
\mathds{Z}^{(-)}
to

\mathds{N}^{(-)}
as desired.

(4)

Given a polynomial functor
H_{\Sigma}

for the signature
\Sigma
,
recall from
6.2
that the labels
A=\mathds{N}

records the generators contained in a shallow term together with their indices:

\flat(\sigma(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}))=\{(1,y_{1}),\ldots,(n,y_{n})\}.

Then the functor interface is given by
w(C)=H\chi_{C}
and

\displaystyle\mathsf{init}(\sigma(0,\ldots,0),f)

\displaystyle=\sigma(1,\ldots,1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{up}(I,\sigma(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}))

\displaystyle=\sigma(b_{1}+(1\in I),\ldots,b_{i}+(i\in I),\ldots,b_{n}+(n\in I)).

Here
b_{i}+(i\in I)
means
b_{i}+1
if
i\in I
and
b_{i}
otherwise.

To verify (
6.1
), let
S\subseteq C\subseteq X
,

t=\sigma(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\in H_{\Sigma}X
with
\sigma
of arity

n
, let
b_{i}=\chi_{C}(y_{i})
, and

I=\{1\leq i\leq n\mid y_{i}\in S\}
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{init}(H_{\Sigma}!(t),\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{1}\cdot\flat(t))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{init}(\sigma(0,\ldots,0),\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{1}(\{(1,y_{1}),\ldots,(n,y_{n})\}))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{init}(\sigma(0,\ldots,0),\{1,\ldots,n\})=\sigma(1,\ldots,1)

\displaystyle=\sigma(\chi_{X}(y_{1}),\ldots,\chi_{X}(y_{n}))=H_{\Sigma}\chi_{X}(t).

\displaystyle\mathsf{up}(\mathsf{fil}_{S}\cdot\flat(t),H_{\Sigma}\chi_{C}(t))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{up}(\mathsf{fil}_{S}(\{(1,y_{1}),\ldots,(n,y_{n})\}),\sigma(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{up}(I,\sigma(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}))

\displaystyle=\sigma(b_{1}+(1\in I),\ldots,b_{i}+(i\in I),\ldots,b_{n}+(n\in I))

\displaystyle=\sigma(\chi_{S}^{C}(y_{1}),\ldots,\chi_{S}^{C}(y_{i}),\ldots,\chi_{S}^{C}(y_{n}))

\displaystyle=H_{\Sigma}\chi_{S}^{C}(t).

In the second last step we use that:

\begin{array}[b]{lcl}y_{i}\in X\setminus C&\Rightarrow&b_{i}+(i\in I)=0+0=0=\chi_{S}^{C}(y_{i}),\\
y_{i}\in C\setminus S&\Rightarrow&b_{i}+(i\in I)=1+0=1=\chi_{S}^{C}(y_{i}),\\
y_{i}\in S&\Rightarrow&b_{i}+(i\in I)=1+1=2=\chi_{S}^{C}(y_{i}).\end{array}

By
Section 2.2
, the interface for Abeliean-group-valued
functors can also be used for monoid-valued functors
M^{(-)}
for
cancellative monoids
M
(as these embed into Abelian groups). In
further work
[
DMSW19
]
, we provide a refinement interface for
monoid-valued functors
M^{(-)}
over unrestricted monoids
M
; this
also yields a refinement interface for
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
as the monoid-valued
functor
(2,\vee,0)^{(-)}
. However, the more general refinement
interface is less efficient than the specific interface for
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}

described in
6.6
; in particular it does not
yield the linear run-time complexity we seek here and require next in

7
.

The next result shows that for every refinement interface the weight
function
w(C)
provides at least as much information as
H\chi_{C}
.

Proposition 6.8
.

For every refinement interface,
H\chi_{C}=H(=1)\cdot\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{update}(\emptyset)\cdot w(C)
.

Proof 6.9
.

The axiom for
\mathsf{update}
and definition of
\mathsf{fil}_{\emptyset}
makes
the following diagram commute:
