{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}A\times W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\times H3\times W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle w(\emptyset),H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C},w(C\setminus\emptyset)\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\chi_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\emptyset!,w(C)\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\flat\cdot\mathsf{fil}_{\emptyset},w(C)\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{update}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(=1)}

Assumption 7
.

From now on, we assume that
H\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
is zippable and given together with
a refinement interface such that
\mathsf{init}
and
\mathsf{update}
run
in linear time,
H3
is linearly ordered, and its elements can be
compared in constant time.

Remark 6.10
.

We implicitly impose some standard assumptions regarding arithmetic
on our computational model, namely that integers can be stored in
atomic memory cells and the usual operations on them, e.g. addition
and comparison, run in constant time.

Example 6.11
.

The refinement interfaces in Examples
6.6

and
6.7
satisfy
7
:

(1)

For all examples using the
\mathsf{val}
-function from
6.7
,
first note that
\mathsf{val}
runs in linear time (with a constant factor of 3,
because
\mathsf{val}
essentially returns three copies of its
input). Hence
\mathsf{update}
runs in linear time if
\mathsf{up}
does.

(2)

For monoid-valued functors
G^{(-)}
over an abelian group
G
,
for
\mathds{N}^{(-)}
and for
\mathcal{D}
, all the operations, including the
summation
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\sum$}e
(see
6.7
.
(1)
),
run in time linear in the size of the input. If the elements

g\in G
have a bounded representation, i.e. fit into boundedly
many memory cells, then so do the elements of
G^{(2)}
; thus
comparing elements of
g_{1},g_{2}\in G^{(2)}
can be performed in
constant time. (By our global assumptions as per
6.10
,
this includes cases where
G
consists of integer or rational
numbers.)

(3)

Given a polynomial functor
H_{\Sigma}
, we assume that operation
symbols
\sigma\in\Sigma
are encoded as integers. As per

6.10
, we can then assume that operation symbols can be
compared in constant time. If the signature has
bounded
arities
(i.e. there is a finite bound on the arity of all
symbols in
\Sigma
), then the maximum arity of operation symbols
present in a given coalgebra
\xi:X\to H_{\Sigma}X
is bounded
independently of
\xi
, so the comparison of the generators in two
shallow
\Sigma
-terms
t_{1},t_{2}\in H_{\Sigma}3
runs in constant time
as well.

(a)

The first parameter of
\mathsf{init}
is of type
H_{\Sigma}1
and
can be encoded by an operation symbol
\sigma
, i.e. by an
integer. Let
t\in H_{\Sigma}2
be fixed. Then we explicitly
implement

\mathsf{init}(\sigma,f)=\begin{cases}\sigma(\smash{\overbrace{1,\ldots,1}^{\mathclap{\text{arity
$\sigma$ many}}}})&\text{if arity}(\sigma)=|f|\\
t&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Both the check and the construction of
\sigma(1,\ldots,1)
run in
linear time in the size of
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathds{N}
, or in constant time in the
second case, since
t
was fixed beforehand.

(b)

In
\mathsf{up}(I,\sigma(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}))
, we cannot naively check
all the
1\in I,\ldots,n\in I
queries, since this would lead to
quadratic run-time. Instead, we precompute all results of possible
queries:

1:

Define an array
\mathsf{elem}
with indices
1\ldots n
, where
each cell stores a value in
2
.

2:

Initialize
\mathsf{elem}
to
0
everywhere.

3:

for

i\in I
with
i\leq n

do

\mathsf{elem}[i]\mathop{:=}1
.

4:

return

\sigma(b_{1}+\mathsf{elem}[1],\ldots,b_{n}+\mathsf{elem}[n])
.

The running time of every line is bound by
|I|+n
.

7.
Efficient Partition Refinement

We now proceed to present
2
in a concrete form that is
parametric in a refinement interface for the coalgebraic type functor

H
. We will prove the correctness in
Section 7.2
and then
analyse the efficient run-time in
Section 7.3
.

We continue to work under
7
.

7.1.
The Concrete Algorithm

In the actual implementation, we need to address edges
explicitly, and so we define the following maps:

Definition 7.1
.

Given a functor
H
, equipped with an encoding, and a map

\xi\colon X\to HX
, the set
E
of
edges
is defined by

E:=\coprod_{x\in X}\coprod_{(a,y)\in A\times X}(\flat\cdot\xi(x))(a,y)

where
(\flat\cdot\xi(x))(a,y)\in\mathds{N}
is considered as a finite
ordinal number. The
encoding
of
\xi
is represented by two
functions (implemented as arrays):

\mathsf{type}=\big(X\xrightarrow{\xi}HX\xrightarrow{H!}H1\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{graph}\colon E\longrightarrow X\times A\times X\ \text{with}\ \mathsf{in}_{x}(\mathsf{in}_{a,y}n)\mapsto(x,a,y).

Remark 7.2
.

For the complexity result, we assume that the partitions

X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P}
and
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q}
are implemented in such
a way that we can add and remove elements in constant time, remove
and create blocks in constant time, and find the surrounding block
of an element
x\in X
or
y\in X
in constant time.

(1)

One possible implementation is by doubly linked lists of the
blocks, where each block is in turn encoded as a doubly linked
list of its elements, and additionally every element
x\in X
,

y\in X
holds a pointer to the corresponding list entry in the
blocks containing them
[
PT87
]
.

(2)

An alternative implementation is the
refinable partition
data structure
[
VF10
]
,
in which the partition is an array of elements and elements in the same block
appear consecutively in the array. So a block of the partition consists of two
indices, denoting the interval in the array of all elements.

Both implementations require
\mathcal{O}(n)
space, where
n
is
the number of elements, but the linked list approach has a much
higher constant factor due to the high number of pointers. Thus, the
implementation of our algorithm
[
DMSW19
]
uses the refinable
partition structure.

The algorithm maintains the following mutable
data structures:

•

An array
\mathsf{toSub}\colon X\to\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}E
, mapping
x\in X
to its outgoing edges ending in the currently processed subblock.

•

A pointer mapping edges to memory addresses:
\mathsf{lastW}\colon E\to\mathds{N}
.
By this pointer, we achieve that edges with a common source node
x\in X
and
a common target block
C\in X/Q
point to the same memory cell holding

w(C,\xi(x))
, as in the concrete
6.3
above.

•

The actual store for weights
\mathsf{deref}\colon\mathds{N}\to W
, into which

\mathsf{lastW}
points.

•

For each block
B\in X/P
a set of markings
\mathsf{mark}_{B}\subseteq B\times\mathds{N}
, that
collects those pairs consisting of a state
x\in B
and the pointer to

w(C,\xi(x))
in the store
\mathsf{deref}
of weights for those
x
that have an outgoing
edge to the current subblock
S
during the splitting operation. Initially,

\mathsf{mark}_{B}
is empty for every newly created block
B
and after each refinement step,

\mathsf{mark}_{B}
is emptied again.

Notation 8
.

In the following we write
e=x\xrightarrow{a}y
in lieu of

\mathsf{graph}(e)=(x,a,y)
. We also overload notation and write the
weight function from the refinement interface of
H
in its
uncurried form as
w\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X\times HX\to W
.

Definition 7.3

(Invariants)
.

Our correctness proof below establishes that the following
properties hold before and after each call to our splitting routine;
we call them
the invariants
:

(1)

The array
\mathsf{toSub}
is empty, i.e. for all
x\in X,\mathsf{toSub}(x)=\emptyset
.

(2)

The pointers in
\mathsf{lastW}
are the same for two edges if and only
if their source nodes and target blocks agree: for every
e_{1}=x_{1}\xrightarrow{a_{1}}y_{1}
and
e_{2}=x_{2}\xrightarrow{a_{2}}y_{2}
we have

\mathsf{lastW}(e_{1})=\mathsf{lastW}(e_{2})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x_{1}=x_{2}\text{ and }[y_{1}]_{\kappa_{Q}}=[y_{2}]_{\kappa_{Q}}.

(3)

The pointers in
\mathsf{lastW}
point to the
correct weights in the store of weights, i.e. for every

e=x\xrightarrow{a}y
in
E
and

C:=[y]_{\kappa_{Q}}\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q}
, we have

w(C,\xi(x))=\mathsf{deref}\cdot\mathsf{lastW}(e).

(4)

For every block
C\in X/Q
, The partition
X/P
is stable
w.r.t.
\chi_{C}
, i.e. for every
x_{1},x_{2}\in B\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P}
and

C\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q}
, we have

(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{C}\cdot\xi)
, cf.
Section 4
.

In the following code listings, we use square brackets for
array lookups and updates in order to emphasize they run in constant
time. We assume that the functions

\mathsf{graph}\colon E\to X\times A\times X
and
\mathsf{type}\colon X\to H1
are
implemented as arrays. In the initialization step, we precompute the additional
static array
\mathsf{pred}\colon X\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}E
,

\mathsf{pred}(y)=\{e\in E\mid e=x\xrightarrow{a}y\}

which holds for every
y\in X
the set of incoming edges.

Sets and bags are implemented as lists. We only insert
elements into sets not yet containing them.

Definition 7.4
.

We say that we
group
(or
split
) a finite set
Z
by a map

f\colon Z\to Z^{\prime}
to indicate that we compute
[-]_{f}
, i.e. we
partition
Z
according the values of its elements under
f
.

This is implemented by first sorting the elements
z\in Z
by a binary encoding
of
f(z)
using any
\mathcal{O}(|Z|\cdot\log|Z|)
sorting algorithm, and
then grouping elements with the same
f(z)
into blocks. In order to
keep the overall complexity for the grouping operations low enough,
one needs to use a possible majority candidate during sorting,
following Valmari and
Franceschinis
[
VF10
]
.

Initialization

1:

for

e\in E
,
e=x\xrightarrow{a}y

do

2:

add
e
to
\mathsf{toSub}[x]
and
\mathsf{pred}[y]

3:

for

x\in X

do

4:

p_{X}\mathop{:=}
new cell in
\mathsf{deref}
containing

\mathsf{init}(\mathsf{type}[x],\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{graph})(\mathsf{toSub}[x]))

5:

for

e\in\mathsf{toSub}[x]

do

\mathsf{lastW}[e]=p_{X}

6:

\mathsf{toSub}[x]:=\emptyset

7:

X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P}\mathop{:=}
group
X
by
\mathsf{type}\colon X\to H1
;

X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q}\mathop{:=}\{X\}
.

Figure 7
.
The initialization procedure.

The algorithm computing the initial partition is listed in

Figure 7
. The first two lines initialize
\mathsf{toSub}(x)

to be the bag of all outgoing edges of
x
and compute

\mathsf{pred}(y)
. Then the loop in lines 3–6 initializes the array

\mathsf{lastW}
, and finally the two partitions
X/P
and
X/Q
are
initialized in line 7.

Lemma 7.5
.

The initialization procedure runs in time
\mathcal{O}(|E|+|X|\cdot\log|X|)
and
the result satisfies the invariants.

Proof 7.6
.

The grouping in line 7 takes
\mathcal{O}(|X|\cdot\log|X|)
time. The first
loop takes
\mathcal{O}(|E|)
steps, and the second one takes
\mathcal{O}(|X|+|E|)
time in total over all
x\in X
since
\mathsf{init}
is assumed to run in linear time. For the invariants:

(1)

By line
6
.

(2)

Let
e_{1}=x_{1}\xrightarrow{a_{1}}y_{1}
and

e_{2}=x_{2}\xrightarrow{a_{2}}y_{2}
. Since

[y_{1}]_{\kappa_{Q}}=[y_{2}]_{\kappa_{Q}}
holds for all
y_{1}
,

y_{2}
, it suffices to show that
\mathsf{lastW}(e_{1})=\mathsf{lastW}(e_{2})
iff
x_{1}=x_{2}
. This holds after the procedure
because
p_{X}
in line
4
is the address of a new memory cell,
whence
\mathsf{lastW}(e_{1})
and
\mathsf{lastW}(e_{2})
are equal iff
they are assigned their value in the same
for
loop in line
5
,
equivalently, if
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{toSub}(x)
for some

x
. Equivalently,
x_{1}=x=x_{2}
because after line
2
,
\mathsf{toSub}(x)
is the bag of
all outgoing edges of
x
.

(3)

First, we have for every
x\in X
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{graph})(\mathsf{toSub}(x))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{graph})(\{e\mid e\in E,e=x\xrightarrow{a}y\}

(line
2
)

\displaystyle=\{a\mid\text{$x\xrightarrow{a}y$ in
$E$}\}

(Def.
7.1
)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{1}\big(\{(a,y)\mid\text{$x\xrightarrow{a}y$ in
$E$}\}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{1}\cdot\flat\cdot\xi(x)

(Def.
7.1
)

where the comprehensions are read as
multiset
comprehensions,
i.e. multiple edges with the same label
a
generate multiple occurrences
of
a
.
Then we use this in the second step below to see that we have for
every
e=x\xrightarrow{a}y
in
E
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{deref}\cdot\mathsf{lastW}(e)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{init}(\mathsf{type}(x),\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{graph})(\mathsf{toSub}(x)))

(lines
4
and
5
)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{init}(\mathsf{type}(x),\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{1}\cdot\flat\cdot\xi(x))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{init}(H!\cdot\xi(x),\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{1}\cdot\flat\cdot\xi(x))

(Def. of
\mathsf{type}
)

\displaystyle=w(X,\xi(x))

by (
6.1
),

and we are done since
X/Q=\{X\}
.

(4)

Since
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{X}\cdot\xi)=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H!\cdot\xi)
, this is
just the way
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P}
is constructed.

The algorithm for a single refinement step is listed in
Figure 8
.
It receives as input the the partition
X/P
, where the kernel
P
is
stable w.r.t.
\kappa_{Q}\colon X\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{{\ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}{{
{
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\ignorespaces\lxSVG@buttcap\ignorespaces{}
{}
{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.72014pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.96007pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}X/Q
, and a subblock
S\subseteq X
contained in
C\in X/Q
. Its task is to split
the blocks in
X/P
in such a way that
P
becomes stable
w.r.t.
\langle\kappa_{Q},\chi_{S}^{C}\rangle
, i.e. stable
w.r.t.
\kappa_{Q}
after
C
has been split into
S
and
C/S
in

X/Q
.

Split
(X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P},S\subseteq X)

1:

\mathsf{M}\mathop{:=}\emptyset\subseteq X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P}\times H3

2:

for

y\in S,e\in\mathsf{pred}[y]

do

3:

x\xrightarrow{a}y\mathop{:=}e

4:

B\mathop{:=}
block with
x\in B\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P}

5:

if

\mathsf{mark}_{B}
is empty
then

6:

w_{C}^{x}\mathop{:=}\mathsf{deref}\cdot\mathsf{lastW}[e]

7:

v_{\emptyset}\mathop{:=}\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{update}(\emptyset,w_{C}^{x})

8:

add
(B,v_{\emptyset})
to
\mathsf{M}

9:

if

\mathsf{toSub}[x]=\emptyset

then

10:

add
(x,\mathsf{lastW}[e])
to
\mathsf{mark}_{B}

11:

add
e
to
\mathsf{toSub}[x]

12:

for

(B,v_{\emptyset})\in\mathsf{M}

do

13:

B_{\neq\emptyset}\mathop{:=}\emptyset\subseteq X\times H3

14:

for

(x,p_{C})
in
\mathsf{mark}_{B}

do

15:

\ell\mathop{:=}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{graph})(\mathsf{toSub}[x])

16:

(w_{S}^{x},v^{x},w_{C\setminus S}^{x})\mathop{:=}\mathsf{update}(\ell,\mathsf{deref}[p_{C}]\mathrlap{)}

17:

\mathsf{deref}[p_{C}]\mathop{:=}w^{x}_{C\setminus S}

18:

p_{S}\mathop{:=}
new cell containing
w_{S}^{x}

19:

for

e\in\mathsf{toSub}[x]

do

\mathsf{lastW}[e]\mathop{:=}p_{S}

20:

\mathsf{toSub}[x]\mathop{:=}\emptyset

21:

if

v^{x}\neq v_{\emptyset}

then

22:

remove
x
from
B

23:

insert
(x,v^{x})
into
B_{\neq\emptyset}

24:

\mathsf{mark}_{B}:=\emptyset

25:

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l}B_{1}\times\{v_{1}\},\ldots,B_{\ell}\times\{v_{\ell}\}\mathop{:=}\\
\quad\text{group }B_{\neq\emptyset}\text{ by }\pi_{2}\colon X\times H3\to H3\end{array}

26:

insert
B_{1},\ldots,B_{\ell}\mathop{:=}
into
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P}

(a)

Collecting predecessor blocks

(b)

Splitting predecessor blocks

Figure 8
.
Refining
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P}
for
S\subseteq C
,
C\in X/Q
,
i.e. making it stable w.r.t.
\chi_{S}^{C}\colon X\to 3
.

In the first part, all blocks
B\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P}

that have an edge into
S
are collected, together with

v_{\emptyset}=H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)\in H3
for all

x\in B
that have no edge into
S
. For each
x\in X
,
\mathsf{toSub}[x]

collects the edges from
x
into
S
. The markings
\mathsf{mark}_{B}
list
those elements
x\in B
that have an edge into
S
, together with one
of the pointers in
\mathsf{lastW}
that point to
w(C,x)
in the store

\mathsf{deref}
of weights.

In the second part, each block
B
with an edge into
S
is split
by
H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi
, cf.
6.3
. First, for
every
(x,p_{C})\in\mathsf{mark}_{B}
, we compute
w(S,x)
,
v^{x}=H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)
,
and
w(C\setminus S,x)
using
\mathsf{update}
. Then, the weight of all edges
x\to C\setminus S
is updated to
w(C\setminus S,x)
and the weight of all edges

x\to S
is stored in a new cell containing
w(S,x)
. For all unmarked
x\in B
,
we know that
H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)=v_{\emptyset}
; so all
x
with

v^{x}=v_{\emptyset}
stay in
B
. All other
x\in B
are removed from

B
and collected in
B_{\neq\emptyset}
together with their value

v^{x}=H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)
, and then we group
B_{\neq\emptyset}

by these values to obtain the new blocks
B_{1},\ldots,B_{\ell}
that we
add to
X/P
.

Now we are ready to combine
Split
from

Figure 8
with what we saw in Sections
4

and
5
and instantiate
2
with the

\mathsf{select}
routine from

4.4
.
(1)
, i.e. we have

q_{i+1}=\mathsf{select}(\kappa_{P_{i}},\kappa_{Q_{i}})\cdot\kappa_{P_{i}}=\chi_{S_{i}}^{C_{i}},

(7.1)

where
2\cdot|S_{i}|\leq|C_{i}|
,
S_{i},C_{i}\subseteq X
in line
(1)
, and
we replace line
(3)
of the algorithm by

X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P_{i+1}}=\textsc{Split}(X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P_{i}},S_{i}),

(
5.1
’)

where the indices are merely intended to facilitate the
analysis.

This yields the following more concretion of

2
:

Algorithm 9
.

Given the
encoding
of an
H
-coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to HX
as
input, do the following:

•

Run
Initialization
(
Figure 7
).

•

Iterate the following steps while
X/P
is properly finer than
X/Q
:

(1)

Pick a subblock
S
in
X/P
, that has at most half of the size of its
compound block
C\in X/Q
, i.e.
S\subseteq C
and
2\cdot|S|\leq|C|
.

(2)

Split
C
into
S
and
C\setminus S
in
X/Q
.

(3)

Call
Split
(X/P,S)
(
Figure 8
).

7.2.
Correctness

Lemma 7.7
.

Assume that the invariants hold. Then after part (a) of
Figure 8
, for the given

S\subseteq C\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q}
we have:

(1)

For all
x\in X
:
\mathsf{toSub}(x)=\{e\in E\mid e=x\xrightarrow{a}y,y\in S\}

(2)

For all
x\in X
:
\mathsf{fil}_{S}\cdot\flat\cdot\xi(x)=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{graph})(\mathsf{toSub}(x))(a)
.

(3)

\mathsf{M}\colon X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P}\rightharpoonup H3
is a partial map
defined by

M(B)=H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x),\qquad\text{if $x\in B$ and there exists an
$e=x\xrightarrow{a}y$ with $y\in S$},

and
M(B)
is undefined otherwise.

(4)

For each
B\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P}
, we have a partial map

\mathsf{mark}_{B}\colon B\rightharpoonup\mathds{N}
defined by

\mathsf{mark}_{B}(x)=\mathsf{lastW}(e)\qquad\text{if there exists some $e=x\xrightarrow{a}y$ with $y\in S$,}

and
\mathsf{mark}_{B}
is undefined otherwise.

(5)

If defined on
x
,
\mathsf{deref}\cdot\mathsf{mark}_{B}(x)=w(C,\xi(x))
.

(6)

If
\mathsf{mark}_{B}
is undefined on
x
, then

\mathsf{fil}_{S}(\flat\cdot\xi(x))=\emptyset
and

H\chi^{C}_{S}\cdot\xi(x)=H\chi^{C}_{\emptyset}\cdot\xi(x)
.

Proof 7.8
.

(1)

By lines
2
and
11
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{toSub}(x)

\displaystyle=\{e\in\mathsf{pred}(y)\mid y\in S,e=x\xrightarrow{a}y\}

\displaystyle=\{e\in E\mid y\in S,e=x\xrightarrow{a}y\}.

(2)

\begin{aligned} \mathsf{fil}_{S}(\flat\cdot\xi(x))(a)&=\sum_{y\in S}(\flat\cdot\xi(x))(a,y)=\sum_{y\in S}|\{e\in E\mid e=x\xrightarrow{a}y\}|\\
&=|\{e\in E\mid e=x\xrightarrow{a}y,y\in S\}|=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{graph})(\mathsf{toSub}(x)).\end{aligned}

(3)

First,
\mathsf{M}
is a partial map since for every block
B
, a
pair
(B,v)
is added to
\mathsf{M}
at most once in line
8
because
when any node
x
from
B
occurs in line 3 for the first time we
have that
\mathsf{mark}_{B}
,
\mathsf{toSub}(x)
are both empty, and they
are both nonempty after line
11
.
By construction
\mathsf{M}
is defined precisely for those blocks

B
which have at least one element
x
with an edge

e=x\xrightarrow{a}y
to
S
. Let

C=[y]_{\kappa_{Q}}\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q}
. Then using
Invariant
(3)
in the second step we see that

\displaystyle M(B)

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{update}(\emptyset,\mathsf{deref}\cdot\mathsf{lastW}(e))

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{update}(\emptyset,w(C,\xi(x)))

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{update}(\mathsf{fil}_{\emptyset}(\flat\cdot\xi(x)),w(C,\xi(x)))

\displaystyle\overset{\mathclap{\eqref{eqSplitterLabels}}}{=}H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)

for the
e=x\xrightarrow{a}y
,
x\in B
,
y\in S
that occurs first in the loop. Since

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi)=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{C}\cdot\xi)
,
Invariant
(4)
proves well-definedness.

(4)

This is precisely how
\mathsf{mark}_{B}
has been constructed; that
it is a partial map follows since for every
x\in X
a pair
(x,n)
is added to
\mathsf{mark}_{B}
at most once in line
10
if

\mathsf{toSub}(x)
is nonempty, and immediately after that

\mathsf{toSub}(x)
becomes nonempty in line
11
.
Well-definedness follows from Invariant
(2)
. Note
that for every
B
on which
\mathsf{M}
is undefined, the list

\mathsf{mark}_{B}
is empty.

(5)

If
\mathsf{mark}_{B}(x)=p_{C}
is defined, then
p_{C}=\mathsf{lastW}(e)

for some
e\in\mathsf{toSub}(x)
, and so
\mathsf{deref}(p_{C})=\mathsf{deref}\cdot\mathsf{lastW}(e)=w(C,\xi(x))
by Invariant
(3)
.

(6)

If
x\in B
is not marked in
B
, then
x
was never mentioned in line 3. Hence,
\mathsf{toSub}(x)=\emptyset
, and we have

\mathsf{fil}_{S}(\flat\cdot\xi(x))(a)=|\{e\in E\mid e=x\xrightarrow{a}y,y\in S\}|=|\mathsf{toSub}(x)|=0.

Furthermore we have

\displaystyle H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)=

\displaystyle\ \pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{update}(\mathsf{fil}_{S}\cdot\flat\cdot\xi(x),w(C,\xi(x)))

by (
6.1
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{update}(\emptyset,w(C,\xi(x)))

as just shown

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{update}(\mathsf{fil}_{\emptyset}\cdot\flat\cdot\xi(x),w(C,\xi(x)))

by definition

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)

by (
6.1
)

Theorem 7.8

(Correctness)
.

If the invariants hold before invoking
Split
, then

(i)

Split
returns the correct partitions, that is,

Split
(X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P},X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q},S\subseteq C\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q})
refines
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P}
by

\smash{H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi\colon X\to H3}
, i.e. all blocks in

X/P
are split by
H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi
so that
P
is replaced by

P\cap\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi)
,
and

(ii)

upon termination of
Split
the invariants hold again.

Proof 7.9
.

(i) We show that every block
B\in X/P
is split by
H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi
, by case distinction whether
B\in\mathsf{M}
:

•

If
B
is not in
\mathsf{M}
, then
\mathsf{mark}_{B}
is undefined everywhere,
so for all
x\in B
, we have by
7.7
(6)

that
H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)=H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)
, which is the
same for all
x\in B
by invariant
(4)
. Hence every
B
not
in
\mathsf{M}
stays unchanged when splitting by
H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi
.

•

If
(B,v_{\emptyset})\in\mathsf{M}
(in line
12
), then
we show that

B_{\neq\emptyset}=\{(x,H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x))\mid x\in B,H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)\neq v_{\emptyset}\}.

This is sufficient for correctness, since

B_{\neq\emptyset}
is split w.r.t. the second component (line

25
) creating new blocks, and since all elements
x\in B

not mentioned in
B_{\neq\emptyset}
stay in
B
.

For this characterization of
B_{\neq\emptyset}
, suppose first that
x\in B
is marked, i.e. we have
p_{C}=\mathsf{mark}_{B}(x)
and lines

14
–
24
are executed. Then we have

\displaystyle(w^{x}_{S},v^{x},w^{x}_{C\setminus S})=\

\displaystyle\mathsf{update}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{graph})(\mathsf{toSub}[x]),\mathsf{deref}[p_{C}])

by line
16

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathsf{update}(\mathsf{fil}_{S}\cdot\flat\cdot\xi(x),w(C,\xi(x)))

by
7.7

(2)
,

(5)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle(w(S,\xi(x)),H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x),w(C\setminus S,\xi(x)))

\displaystyle\text{by \eqref{eqSplitterLabels}}.

Thus, if
H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)=v_{\emptyset}
,
(x,v^{x})
is not added to

B_{\neq\emptyset}
(line
21
), and otherwise
x
is
correctly removed from
B
and added to
B_{\neq\emptyset}
in
line
24
.

Now suppose that
x\in B
is not marked, then
x
is not added to

B_{\neq\emptyset}
and so we have to show that
v_{\emptyset}=H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)
. We have
v_{\emptyset}=H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x^{\prime})

for some
x^{\prime}\in B
by
7.7
(3)
and

H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)=H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)

by
7.7
(6)
. Since
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi^{C}_{\emptyset}\cdot\xi)=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{C}\cdot\xi)
and
(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{C}\cdot\xi)

by invariant
(4)
, we have
H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x^{\prime})=H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)
. Thus,

v_{\emptyset}=H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x^{\prime})=H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x)=H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x).

(ii) We denote the former values of

P,Q,\mathsf{deref},\mathsf{lastW}
using the subscript
{\mathsf{old}}
.

(1)

It is easy to see that
\mathsf{toSub}(x)
becomes nonempty in
line 11 only for marked
x
, and for those
x
it is emptied
again in line 20.

(2)

Take
e_{1}=x_{1}\xrightarrow{a_{1}}y_{1}
,
e_{2}=x_{2}\xrightarrow{a_{2}}y_{2}
.

\Rightarrow

Assume
\mathsf{lastW}(e_{1})=\mathsf{lastW}(e_{2})
.
If
\mathsf{lastW}(e_{1})=p_{S}
is assigned in line

19
for some marked
x
, then

\mathsf{lastW}(e_{2})
must be assigned to
p_{S}
in the same

for
loop since
p_{S}
is the address of a new memory cell
(line
18
). Hence,
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{toSub}(x)
,
which implies that
x_{1}=x=x_{2}
and

y_{1},y_{2}\in S\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q}
.
Otherwise,
\mathsf{lastW}(e_{1})=\mathsf{lastW}_{\mathsf{old}}(e_{1})
and so
\mathsf{lastW}_{\mathsf{old}}(e_{1})=\mathsf{lastW}_{\mathsf{old}}(e_{2})
and the desired property follows from the
invariant for
\mathsf{lastW}_{\mathsf{old}}
.

\Leftarrow

If
x_{1}=x_{2}
and

y_{1},y_{2}\in D\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q}
, where recall that

X/Q=X/Q_{\mathsf{old}}\setminus\{C\}\cup\{S,C\setminus S\}
, then we
perform a case distinction on
D
. If
D=S
, then

e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{toSub}(x)

(
7.7
(1)
) and so both entries in
the array are set to the same value

\mathsf{lastW}(e_{1})=\mathsf{lastW}(e_{2})=p_{S}
in
line
19
. If
D\neq S
, then

e_{1},e_{2}\not\in\mathsf{toSub}(x)
and so
\mathsf{lastW}[e_{1}]
and

\mathsf{lastW}[e_{2}]
stay unchanged. Hence, we have

\mathsf{lastW}(e_{1})=\mathsf{lastW}_{\mathsf{old}}(e_{1})=\mathsf{lastW}_{\mathsf{old}}(e_{2})=\mathsf{lastW}(e_{2}).

(3)

Let
e=x\xrightarrow{a}y
,

D:=[y]_{\kappa_{Q}}\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q}
. We again perform a
case distinction on
D
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}D=S&\Rightarrow&\mathsf{deref}\cdot\mathsf{lastW}(e)=\mathsf{deref}(p_{S})=w_{S}^{x}=w(S,\xi(x)),\\
D=C\setminus S&\Rightarrow&\mathsf{deref}\cdot\mathsf{lastW}(e)=\mathsf{deref}(p_{C})=w_{C\setminus S}^{x}=w(C\setminus S,\xi(x)),\\
D\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{\mathsf{old}}}\setminus\{C\}&\Rightarrow&\mathsf{deref}\cdot\mathsf{lastW}(e)=\mathsf{deref}_{\mathsf{old}}\cdot\mathsf{lastW}(e)_{\mathsf{old}}=w(D,\xi(x)).\end{array}

Note that the first equation in the second case holds due to lines
10
and
14
.
For the first two cases note that

w_{S}^{x}=w(S,\xi(x))
and

w_{C\setminus S}^{x}=w(C\setminus S,\xi(x))
by line

16
,
7.7
, items
(2)

and
(5)
, and by the axiom for
\mathsf{update}
in
(
6.1
).

(4)

Take
x_{1},x_{2}\in B^{\prime}\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P}
and
D\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q}
and let
B:=[x_{1}]_{P_{\mathsf{old}}}=[x_{2}]_{P_{\mathsf{old}}}\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P_{\mathsf{old}}}
. By case distinction on
\mathsf{M}(B)
we first
show that
H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x_{1})=H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x_{2})
.

(a)

If
\mathsf{M}(B)
is defined, then

H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x_{1})=H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x_{2})
–
for otherwise
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
would have been put into different blocks in line

25
.

(b)

If
\mathsf{M}(B)
is undefined, then
\mathsf{mark}_{B}
is undefined
everywhere, in particular for
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. Then, by

7.7
(6)
we have

H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi(x_{i})=H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x_{i})
for
i=1,2
.
Since
C\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{\mathsf{old}}}
and

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi^{C}_{\emptyset}\cdot\xi)=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{C}\cdot\xi)
,
we have

H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x_{1})=H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x_{2})
by invariant

(4)
, and so

(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi)
.

We can conclude the invariant by another case distinction on
D
:

\begin{array}[b]{lll}D=S&\Rightarrow&(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi)\subseteq\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(\overbrace{H(=2)\cdot H\chi_{S}^{C}}^{H\chi_{S}=H\chi_{D}}\cdot\xi),\\
D=C\setminus S&\Rightarrow&(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{S}^{C}\cdot\xi)\subseteq\smash{\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(\underbrace{H(=1)\cdot H\chi_{S}^{C}}_{H\chi_{C\setminus S}=H\chi_{D}}\cdot\xi)},\\
D\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{\mathsf{old}}}\setminus\{C\}&\Rightarrow&(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{D}\cdot\xi),\end{array}

where we use
Section 2.1
(1)
and where the last statement holds
by invariant
(4)
for
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{\mathsf{old}}}
.

Corollary 7.10
.

9
computes the simple quotient of a given finite coalgebra

\xi\colon X\to HX
.

Proof 7.11
.

Indeed, by
Theorem 10
(i) and (
7.1
) we see
that (
’
‣
7.1
) is equivalent to
(
5.1
):

P_{i+1}=P_{i}\cap\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{S_{i}}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi)=P_{i}\cap\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(Hq_{i+1}\cdot\xi).

By
5.26
, this is equivalent to the original line

(3)
in
2
,
since the employed “select the smaller half” routine respects
compound blocks and
H
is zippable.
The desired result thus follows from
Theorem 4
.

7.3.
Efficiency

Having established correctness of
Split
, we next
analyse its time complexity. We first analyse lines

\ref{algoFirstLine}-\ref{algoLastLine}
, then the complexity of the
grouping operation in line
25
, and finally the
overall complexity of the algorithm, accumulating the time for all

Split
invocations.

Lemma 7.12
.

Lines
\ref{algoFirstLine}-\ref{algoLastLine}
in
Split
run
in time
\smash{\mathcal{O}(\sum_{y\in S}|\mathsf{pred}(y)|)}
.

Proof 7.13
.

The loop in line
2
has
\sum_{y\in S}|\mathsf{pred}(y)|
iterations, each consisting of constantly many
operations taking constant time.
Since each loop appends one element to some initially empty

\mathsf{toSub}(x)
, we have

\sum_{y\in S}|\mathsf{pred}(y)|=\sum_{x\in X}|\mathsf{toSub}(x)|.

In the body of the loop starting in line
14
, the
only statements not running in constant time are

\ell\mathop{:=}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{graph})(\mathsf{toSub}(x))
(line

15
),
\mathsf{update}(\ell,\mathsf{deref}(p_{C}))
(line

16
), and the loop in line

19
; each of these require time linear in the
length of
\mathsf{toSub}(x)
. The loop in line
14

has at most one iteration per
x\in X
. Hence, since each
x
is
contained in at most one block
B
from line
12
,
the overall complexity of line
12
to

24
is at most

\sum_{x\in X}|\mathsf{toSub}(x)|=\sum_{y\in S}|\mathsf{pred}(y)|
,
as desired.

In the grouping operation in line
25
, it is not enough to group
the elements using a sorting algorithm. Instead we need to preprocess the
elements and extract a possible majority candidate.

Definition 7.14
.

When grouping
Z
by
f\colon Z\to Z^{\prime}
we call an element

p\in Z^{\prime}
a
possible majority candidate
(PMC) if either

|\{z\in Z\mid f(z)=p\}|\geq|\{z\in Z\mid f(z)\neq p\}|

(7.2)

or if no element in
Z^{\prime}
fulfilling (
7.2
) exists.

A PMC can be computed in linear time
[
Bac86
, Sect. 4.3.3]
. When
grouping
Z
by
f
using a PMC, one first determines a PMC
p\in Z^{\prime}
, and then one only sorts and groups
\{z\mid f(z)\neq p\}
by
f
using an
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\log n)
sorting algorithm.

Remark 7.15
.

In the following lemmas we again index data by the iterations
1\leq i\leq k
in
9
. That
means we consider
S_{i}\subseteq C_{i}\in X/Q_{i+1}
such that

X/Q_{i+1}=X/Q_{i}\setminus\{C_{i}\}\cup\{S_{i},C_{i}\setminus S_{i}\}.

(7.3)

Lemma 7.16
.

Summing over all
Split

invocations in
9
, the
total time spent on grouping
B_{\neq\emptyset}
using a PMC is in

\mathcal{O}(|E|\cdot\log|X|)
.

The proof is a generalization of that for the weighted setting of
Valmari and Franceschinis
[
VF10
, Lemma 5]
:

Proof 7.17
.

We shall prove that for
S_{i}\subseteq C_{i}\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}}
,
0\leq i<k
, in
7.15
, the overall time spent on grouping the

B_{\neq\emptyset}
in all the runs of
Split
is in
\mathcal{O}(|E|\cdot\log|X|)
. The partition returned by the algorithm is
X/P_{k}=X/Q_{k}
and is
obtained after
Split
has been called
k
-many times.

In the first part of the proof, we define subsets

\mathcal{L}_{B},\mathcal{M}_{B}\subseteq B_{\neq\emptyset}
which are
generalized version of the left-hand and middle subblocks of a block

B
in
Figure 6
for a
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
-coalgebra, i.e. those
state with successors in
S
but not in
C\setminus S
and those with
successors in both. We then give an equivalent characterization of

\mathcal{L}_{B}
and
\mathcal{M}_{B}
.

In the second part, we use this characterization and a PMC to argue
that sorting each
B_{\neq\emptyset}
is bounded by

2\cdot|\mathcal{M}_{B}|\cdot\log(2\cdot|\mathcal{M}_{B}|)
. Since
we assume that comparing two elements of
H3
runs in constant time,
the time needed for sorting amounts to the number of comparisons
needed while sorting, i.e.
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\log n)
many.

Finally, in the third part, we use this to obtain the desired overall complexity.

(1)

For a
(B,v_{\emptyset})\in\mathsf{M}
in the iteration
i
consider

B_{\neq\emptyset}
. We define

•

the
left block

\mathcal{L}_{B}^{i}:=\{x\in B\mid H\chi_{C_{i}}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi(x)=H\chi_{S_{i}}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi(x)\neq H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi(x)\}
, and

•

the
middle block

\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}:=\{x\in B\mid H\chi_{C_{i}}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi(x)\neq H\chi_{S_{i}}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi(x)\neq H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi(x)\}
.

Now, first note that

(x,v^{x})\in B_{\neq\emptyset}\ \text{iff}\ v^{x}=H\chi_{S_{i}}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi(x)\neq v_{\emptyset},\qquad\text{and}\qquad v_{\emptyset}=H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C}\cdot\xi(x),

(7.4)

where the latter holds by

7.7
(3)
. This implies that
the set of first components of the pairs in
B_{\neq\emptyset}
is

\mathcal{L}_{B}^{i}\cup\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}
. If
x\in B
has no edge to

{S_{i}}
, then it is not marked, and so

H\chi_{S_{i}}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi(x)=H\chi_{\emptyset}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi(x)
, by

7.7
(6)
; by contraposition,
every
x\in\mathcal{L}_{B}^{i}\cup\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}
has some edge into

{S_{i}}
. We can make a similar observation for
C_{i}\setminus S_{i}
. If
x\in B
has no edge to
{C_{i}}\setminus{S_{i}}
, then

\mathsf{fil}_{S_{i}}(\flat\cdot\xi(x))=\mathsf{fil}_{C_{i}}(\flat\cdot\xi(x))

by the definition of

\mathsf{fil}_{S_{i}}
, and therefore we have:

\begin{array}[]{r@{}c@{}l@{}}H\chi_{C_{i}}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi(x)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqSplitterLabels}}}{{=}}&\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{update}(\mathsf{fil}_{C_{i}}(\flat\cdot\xi(x)),w({C_{i}},\xi(x)))\\
&=&\pi_{2}\cdot\mathsf{update}(\mathsf{fil}_{S_{i}}(\flat\cdot\xi(x)),w({C_{i}},\xi(x)))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqSplitterLabels}}}{{=}}H\chi_{S_{i}}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi(x).\end{array}\hskip-14.22636pt

By contraposition, all
x\in\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}
have an edge to

{C_{i}}\setminus{S_{i}}
.

Note that

\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{C_{i}}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi)=\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}(H\chi_{C_{i}}\cdot\xi)
; indeed, to see this use
Section 2.1
(2)
,
that
H
preserves monomorphisms, and that
\chi_{C_{i}}^{C_{i}}=m\cdot\chi_{C_{i}}
, where
m\colon 2\cong\{0,2\}\hookrightarrow\{0,1,2\}=3
is the inclusion map.
Since
\mathcal{L}_{B}^{i}\subseteq B\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{P_{i}}
and

C_{i}\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}}
, we conclude from invariant
(4)

that there is an
\ell^{i}_{B}\in H3
such that

\ell^{i}_{B}=H\chi_{C_{i}}^{C_{i}}\cdot\xi(x)
for all

x\in B
. Using (
7.4
) we therefore obtain

\mathcal{L}_{B}^{i}=\{x\mid(x,v^{x})\in B_{\neq\emptyset},v^{x}=\ell^{i}_{B}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}=\{x\mid(x,v^{x})\in B_{\neq\emptyset},v^{x}\neq\ell^{i}_{B}\}.

We have also seen that every
x\in\mathcal{L}_{B}^{i}
has an edge to

S_{i}
and every
x\in\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}
has both an edge to
S_{i}

and
C_{i}\setminus S_{i}
.

(2)

We prove that sorting

B_{\neq\emptyset}
in the iteration
i
is bound by

2\cdot|\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}|\cdot\log(2\cdot|\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}|)

by case distinction on the possible majority candidate:

•

If
\ell_{B}^{i}
is the possible majority
candidate, then the sorting of
B_{\neq\emptyset}
sorts
precisely
\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}
which indeed amounts
to

|\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}|\cdot\log(|\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}|)\leq 2\cdot|\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}|\cdot\log(2\cdot|\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}|).

•

If
\ell_{B}^{i}
is not the possible
majority candidate, then
|\mathcal{L}_{B}^{i}|\leq|\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}|
.
In this case sorting
B_{\neq\emptyset}
is bounded by

(|\mathcal{L}_{B}^{i}|+|\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}|)\cdot\log(|\mathcal{L}_{B}^{i}|+|\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}|)\leq 2\cdot|\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}|\cdot\log(2\cdot|\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}|).

(3)

Finally, we show that we obtain the desired overall time
complexity. Let the number of blocks to which
x
has an edge be
denoted by

\displaystyle\sharp^{i}_{Q}(x)

\displaystyle=|\{D\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i}}\mid e=x\xrightarrow{a}{y},y\in D\}|,\quad i\geq 0.

Clearly, this number is bounded by the number of outgoing edges of

x
, i.e.
\sharp^{i}_{Q}(x)\leq|\flat\cdot\xi(x)|
, and so

\sum_{x\in X}\sharp_{Q}^{i}(x)\leq\sum_{x\in X}|\flat\cdot\xi(x)|=|E|.

Define

\displaystyle\sharp_{\mathcal{M}}^{i}(x)

\displaystyle=|\{0\leq j<i\mid x\text{ is in some }\mathcal{M}_{B}^{j}\}|,\quad i\geq 0.

If in the iteration
i\geq 0
,
x
is in a middle block
\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}
,
then
\sharp_{\mathcal{M}}^{i+1}(x)=\sharp_{\mathcal{M}}^{i}(x)+1
and

\sharp_{Q}^{i+1}(x)=\sharp_{Q}^{i}(x)+1
, by (
7.3
) and since
x

has both an edge to
S_{i}
and
C_{i}\setminus S_{i}
. Otherwise, if
x
is not
in any middle block in iteration
i
, then
\sharp_{\mathcal{M}}^{i+1}(x)=\sharp_{\mathcal{M}}^{i}(x)
and
\sharp_{Q}^{i+1}(x)\geq\sharp_{Q}^{i}(x)
.
This implies that for all
i\geq 0
,
\sharp_{\mathcal{M}}^{i}(x)\leq\sharp_{Q}^{i}(x)
, and therefore

\sum_{x\in X}\sharp_{\mathcal{M}}^{i}(x)\leq\sum_{x\in X}\sharp_{Q}^{i}(x)\leq|E|.

Let
T
denote the total number of middle blocks

\mathcal{M}_{B}^{i}
,
0\leq i<k
, such that
B
is contained in

\mathsf{M}
in iteration
i
,
and let
\mathcal{M}_{t}
,
1\leq t\leq T
, be the
t^{\text{th}}

middle block. The sum of the sizes of all middle blocks is the
same as the number of times each
x\in X
was contained in a
middle block, i.e.

\sum_{t=1}^{T}|\mathcal{M}_{t}|=\sum_{x\in X}\sharp_{\mathcal{M}}^{k}(x)\leq|E|.

Using the previous bounds and
|\mathcal{M}_{t}|\leq|X|
, we now obtain

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq\ }\sum_{t=1}^{T}2\cdot|\mathcal{M}_{t}|\cdot\log(2\cdot|\mathcal{M}_{t}|)\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}2\cdot|\mathcal{M}_{t}|\cdot\log(2\cdot|X|)=2\cdot\big(\sum_{t=1}^{T}|\mathcal{M}_{t}|\big)\cdot\log(2\cdot|X|)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot|E|\cdot\log(2\cdot|X|)=2\cdot|E|\cdot\log(|X|)+2\cdot|E|\cdot\log(2)\in\mathcal{O}\big(|E|\cdot\log(|X|)\big).

Lemma 7.18
.

(1)

For each
y\in X
,
|\{i<k\mid y\in S_{i}\}|\leq\log_{2}|X|+1
.

(2)

The total run-time of all invocations of

\textsc{Split}(X/P_{i},S_{i})
,
0\leq i<k
, is in
\mathcal{O}(|E|\cdot\log|X|)
.

Proof 7.19
.

(1)

We know from
4.6
that
Q_{j}
is finer than

Q_{i}
for every
j>i
. Moreover, by (
7.3
), we have

S_{i}\in X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i+1}}
. For every
i<j
with
y\in S_{i}

and
y\in S_{j}
, we know that
C_{j}\subseteq S_{i}
since
C_{j}
is
the block containing
y
in the refinement
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{j}}

of
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{Q_{i+1}}
in which
S_{i}
contains
y
. Hence,
we have
2\cdot|S_{j}|\leq|C_{j}|\leq|S_{i}|
. Now let

i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}
be all the elements in

\{i<k\mid y\in S_{i}\}
. Since

y\in S_{i_{1}},\ldots,y\in S_{i_{n}}
, we have

2^{n-1}\cdot|S_{i_{n}}|\leq|S_{i_{1}}|
. Thus

\displaystyle|\{i<k\mid y\in S_{i}\}|=n

\displaystyle=\log_{2}(2^{n-1})+1

\displaystyle\leq\log_{2}(2^{n-1}\cdot|S_{i_{n}}|)+1\leq\log_{2}|S_{i_{1}}|+1\leq\log_{2}|X|+1,

where the last inequality holds since
S_{i_{1}}\subseteq X
.

(2)

By
7.12
, the first term below gives the total
run-time in the
\mathcal{O}
-calculus, and we continue to reason in that calculus
(note that the inner sums on the right-hand side of the first line
are indexed by
S_{i}
, whence the
\ni
-symbol):

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq i<k}\sum_{y\in S_{i}}|\mathsf{pred}(y)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in X}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i<k\\
S_{i}\ni y\end{subarray}}|\mathsf{pred}(y)|=\sum_{y\in X}\big(|\mathsf{pred}(y)|\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i<k\\
S_{i}\ni y\end{subarray}}1\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in X}\big(|\mathsf{pred}(y)|\cdot|\{i<k\mid y\in S_{i}\}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{y\in X}\big(|\mathsf{pred}(y)|\cdot(\log|X|+1)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\sum_{y\in X}|\mathsf{pred}(y)|\big)\cdot(\log|X|+1)

\displaystyle=|E|\cdot(\log|X|+1),

where the inequality holds by the first part of our lemma.

By Lemmas
7.5
and
7.18
(2)
, we
obtain our main result:

Theorem 7.19
.

Given a zippable functor
H\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
with a refinement interface,
whose
\mathsf{update}
and
\mathsf{init}
functions can be computed in linear time,

9
computes the quotient modulo
behavioural equivalence of a given coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to HX
with

n=|X|
states and
m=\sum_{x\in X}|\flat\cdot\xi(x)|
edges in time

\mathcal{O}((m+n)\cdot\log n)
.

If the coalgebra is not too sparse, i.e. every state has at
least one in- or outgoing edge,
2\cdot m\geq n
, then the complexity
is
\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\log n)
, the bound typically seen in the literature
for efficient algorithms for bisimilarity minimization of transition
systems
\xi\colon X\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X
or weighted systems

\xi\colon X\to\mathds{R}^{(X)}
. Unlike those algorithms we do not directly
admit an initial partition as a parameter; but switching from a
functor
G
to
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\times G

(cf.
4.12
) we can equip the generic
algorithm with this additional parameter while maintaining the same

\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\log n)
complexity:

Remark 7.20
.

There are two ways to handle functors of type
H=X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\times G
.
First, we can modify the functor interface as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}G1&\mapsto&X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\times G1\\
W&\mapsto&X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\times W\\
\end{array}\qquad\qquad\begin{array}[]{lll}\flat&\mapsto&X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\times GX\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}GX\xrightarrow{\flat}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times X)\\
\mathsf{init}&\mapsto&\mathsf{id}_{X}\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\times\mathsf{init}\\
\end{array}

\mathsf{update}
is replaced by the following

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(A)\!\times\!W)\!\times\!X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W\!\times\!G3\!\times\!W)\times X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(A)\!\times\!(X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\!\times\!W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\!\times\!W)\times(X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\!\times\!G3)\times(X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\!\times\!W)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{update}\times X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the first and the last morphism are the obvious ones. A second approach is
to decompose the functor into
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\times(-)

and
G
, moving to the multisorted setting, see

Section 8
for more details, in particular
8.21
. Both
methods have no effect on the complexity.

Example 7.21
.

As instances of our algorithm, we obtain the following standard examples for partition refinement algorithms:

(1)

For
H=X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
, we obtain the
classical Paige-Tarjan algorithm
[
PT87
]
(with
initial partition
X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}
), with the same
complexity
\mathcal{O}((m+n)\cdot\log n)
.

Recall from
Section 2.2
that the equivalence
computed by the Paige-Tarjan algorithm – bisimilarity – is
precisely behavioural equivalence for the powerset functor

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
, also when an initial partition is taken into account
(
4.12
).
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
and

X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
are zippable functors
(
5.9
,
5.5
), and we have
refinement interfaces for them (
6.6
,

7.20
) that realize the desired time
complexity bound (
6.11
). So the instantiation of

9
computes the bisimilarity relation on an
input coalgebra with
n
states and
m
edges in time

\mathcal{O}((m+n)\cdot\log n)
by
Theorem 11
.

The instantiation of
9
for the refinement interface of

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
is nearly identical to the Paige-Tarjan
algorithm
[
PT87
]
, informally described in

6.3
. For example, both maintain references from the
edges to the integer counters. However, the
(three way-)
split of a block
is simpler in the specific implementation than in
Split
(Line

25
), because
|\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}3|=8
is finite and in fact in Line

25
at most three different values can occur (namely the
three cases from
6.3
).

For an example run, see
Section 3
and
1(a)
.

(2)

For
HX=X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}\times\mathds{R}^{(X)}
, we solve
Markov chain lumping with an initial partition

X\mkern-1.5mu/\mkern-1.5mu{\mathcal{I}}
in time
\mathcal{O}((m+n)\cdot\log n)
,
like the best known algorithm by Valmari and
Franceschinis
[
VF10
]
:

Coalgebraic behavioural equivalence for
\mathds{R}^{(-)}
captures precisely
weighted bisimilarity (
Section 2.2
), and
\mathds{R}^{(-)}
is
zippable (
5.9
) and has a refinement interface
(
6.7
) with the required complexity bounds
(
6.11
). So the instantiation of
9

computes weighted bisimilarity for an input coalgebra with
n
states and

m
edges in
\mathcal{O}((m+n)\cdot\log n)
by
Theorem 11
.

The algorithm by Valmari and Franceschinis
[
VF10
]
is essentially
that of
Split
(
Figure 8
), after making
simplifications using properties of
\mathds{R}^{(-)}
. For instance, Valmari and
Franceschinis do not need to keep the accumulated weights from states to
blocks in memory since the weights can be computed from the labels
within the first loop of
Split
.

For an example run, see
Section 3
and
1(b)
.

(3)

The functor
H=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}
has a refinement interface
(
6.7
) with the desired run-time
(
6.11
), and thus
9
computes
behavioural equivalence on a
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}
-coalgebra with
n
edges
and
m
states in time
\mathcal{O}((m+n)\cdot\log n)
.

Partition refinement for “undirected”
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}
-coalgebras is known as

colour refinement
and called the
1-dimensional
Weisfeiler-Lehman Algorithm
(WL), where undirected means that

x\xrightarrow{n}y
iff
y\xrightarrow{n}x
. Colour refinement is an
important subroutine in graph isomorphism checking (and was originally
conjectured to check graph isomorphism, see
e.g.
[
CFI92
,
Wei76
,
BBG17
]
). Its input is an
undirected graph
(V,E)
, i.e.
E
is a set of two-element subsets of
V
.
Then color refinement is just partition refinement on the
\mathcal{B}
-coalgebra

\xi\colon V\to\mathcal{B}V
defined by
\xi(u)(v)=1
if
\{u,v\}\in E
and
\xi(u)(v)=0
otherwise (i.e. one introduces two directed edges
per undirected edge as usual). Our algorithm runs in
\mathcal{O}((m+n)\cdot\log n)
,
matching the run-time of the optimal algorithm by Berkholz, Bonsma, and
Grohe
[
BBG17
]
, and improving the run-time of
\mathcal{O}(m\cdot n)
of
a previous algorithm
[
SSvL+11
]
.

(4)

Hopcroft’s classical automata minimization
[
Hop71
]

is obtained by
HX=2\times X^{A}
, with running time
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\log n)

for the binary input alphabet
A=\{0,1\}
(Gries
[
Gri73
]
and
Knuutila
[
Knu01
]
present this algorithm for arbitrary
finite input alphabets
A
which are not fixed but part of
the input of minimization).

For a fixed finite alphabet
A
, the functor
HX=2\times X^{A}
–
and also any other polynomial functor with bounded arity – is
zippable (
5.5
) and has a refinement interface
(
6.7
) fulfilling the complexity bound
(
6.11
). So
9
computes
behavioural equivalence for an input coalgebra with
n
states and

m
edges in time
\mathcal{O}((m+n)\cdot\log n)
by

Theorem 11
. The functor encoding for polynomial
functors (
6.2
) encodes one occurrence of a

k
-ary operation symbol by
k
edges. Since we assume the
signature to have bounded arity, the maximal
k
is independent
of the coalgebra size and thus a constant factor, and so

m\leq\max(k)\cdot n\in\mathcal{O}(n)
. So the run-time of

9
simplifies to
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\log n)
.

Note that the mentioned automata minimization algorithms

[
Hop71
]
are different from
9
because
they perform a refinement step for each input symbol
a\in A
,
whereas
Split
refines a block w.r.t. all labels, since it
considers all edges into the subblock
S\subseteq C
of interest.

If
A
is variable and part of the input, we can fit the minimization in the
present generic framework via the following
Section 8
on
composite functors and multisorted sets (see
8.21
below).

8.
Modularity via Multisorted Coalgebra

We next describe how to minimize systems that mix different transition
types. For example, recall from
Section 2.2
(5)

that Segala systems mix non-deterministic and probabilistic branching
in a way that makes them coalgebras for the composite functor

X\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{D}(A\times X))
(or

X\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times\mathcal{D}X)
in the case of simple Segala
systems, respectively). For our purposes, such functors raise the
problem that zippable functors are not closed under composition. In
the following, we show how to deal with this issue by moving from
composite functors to multisorted coalgebras in the spirit of previous
work on modularity in coalgebraic
logic
[
SP11
]
. Subsequently, the arising
multisorted coalgebras are transformed back to singlesorted coalgebras
by coproduct formation.

8.1.
Explicit intermediate states via multisortedness

The transformation from coalgebras for composite functors
into multisorted coalgebras is best understood by example:

Example 8.1
.

The functor
TX=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}X\times\mathcal{D}(A\times X))
can be visualized as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times(-)}
X_{2}
X_{3}
X_{4}
X_{5}
X
X
X

where we label the inner connections with fresh names
X_{2}
,
X_{3}
,

X_{4}
,
X_{5}
. From this visualization, we derive a functor

\bar{T}\colon\mathsf{Set}^{5}\to\mathsf{Set}^{5}
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{}l@{\ }l@{\ }l@{\ }l@{\ }l}\bar{T}\,(&X,&X_{2},&X_{3},&X_{4},&X_{5}\hskip 16.00008pt)=\\
(&\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X_{2},&X_{3}\times X_{4},&\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}X,&\mathcal{D}X_{5},&A\times X).\end{array}

Formal definitions following
[
SP11
]
are
as follows.

Definition 8.2
.

Given a set
\mathcal{H}
of
mono-preserving and finitary functors
H\colon\mathsf{Set}^{k}\to\mathsf{Set}
(with
possibly different arities
k<\omega
), let
T\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}

be a functor generated by the grammar

T::=(-)\mid H(T,\ldots,T)

where
H
ranges over
\mathcal{H}
and
(-)
is the argument, i.e.
T=(-)

is the functor
TX=X
. By inspecting the structure of such a term
T
, we
can define a functor
\bar{T}\colon\mathsf{Set}^{n}\to\mathsf{Set}^{n}
, where
n
is the number
of non-leaf subterms of
T
(i.e. subterms of
T
including
T
itself but
not
(-)
). Let
f
be a bijection from non-leaf subterms of
T
to natural
numbers
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, with
f(T)=1
, and write
f(-)=1
to simplify
notation (still,
f^{-1}(1)=T
). The
flattening
of
T
is
\bar{T}\colon\mathsf{Set}^{n}\to\mathsf{Set}^{n}
, given by

(\bar{T}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))_{i}=H(X_{f(G_{1})},\ldots,X_{f(G_{k})})\quad\text{where }f^{-1}(i)=H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}).

Intuitively speaking, we introduce a sort for each wire in
the visualization but identify the outermost wires (labelled
X
in
Example
8.1
). Note that we keep track of duplicates, so
e.g.
TX=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X
has three subterms:
(-)\times(-)
, the left
hand and the right hand
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
, so
n=3
and
\bar{T}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})=(X_{2}\times X_{3},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X_{1},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X_{1})
.

In the remainder of this section we write
T
for functors defined
according to the grammar in
8.2
but continue to
write
H
for functors in general (in particular for elements
of
\mathcal{H}
).

Example 8.3
.

In
8.1
the functor

T
is built from the set
\mathcal{H}
of functors containing

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}},\mathcal{D},A\times(-)\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}\qquad\times\colon\mathsf{Set}^{2}\to\mathsf{Set},

and the term
T=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(-)\times\mathcal{D}(A\times(-)))

has the
following non-leaf subterms, implicitly defining the bijection
f
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}1.~\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(-)\times\mathcal{D}(A\times(-)))&2.~\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(-)\times\mathcal{D}(A\times(-))&3.~\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(-)\\
4.~\mathcal{D}(A\times(-))&5.~A\times(-).\end{array}

Then,
\bar{T}\colon\mathsf{Set}^{5}\to\mathsf{Set}^{5}
is defined by

\bar{T}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4},X_{5})=(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X_{2},X_{3}\times X_{4},\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}X_{1},\mathcal{D}X_{5},A\times X_{1}).

Now a coalgebra for the flattening
\bar{T}
of a functor term
T
is a family of maps

\xi_{i}\colon X_{i}\to H(X_{f(G_{1})},\ldots,X_{f(G_{k})})\quad\text{where }f^{-1}(i)=H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}).

For example, given a
T
-coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to TX
, the morphism

(\xi,\mathsf{id},\ldots,\mathsf{id})
in
\mathsf{Set}^{n}
is a coalgebra for the flattening

\bar{T}\colon\mathsf{Set}^{n}\to\mathsf{Set}^{n}
of
T
. Note that this defines the
first sort to be
X
and implicitly defines the other sorts. This
mapping defines a functor

\mathsf{Pad}\colon\mathsf{Coalg}(T)\to\mathsf{Coalg}(\bar{T})
, which is a fully
faithful right-adjoint
[
SP11
]
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(\bar{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Pad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Comp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

The left adjoint
\mathsf{Comp}
composes the component maps of a
multisorted coalgebra in a suitable way as we now explain. For a given

\bar{T}
-coalgebra
(\bar{X},\bar{\xi})
with
\bar{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})
we first define for every subterm
T^{\prime}
of
T
a map

c_{T^{\prime}}\colon X_{f(T^{\prime})}\to T^{\prime}X_{1}

by induction as follows: for
T^{\prime}=(-)
put

c_{(-)}=\mathsf{id}_{X_{1}}\colon X_{f(-)}=X_{1}\to X_{1}
, and for
T^{\prime}=H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k})
,
c_{T^{\prime}}
is the following map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$X_{f(T^{\prime})}=\,$}X_{f(H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(X_{f(G_{1})},\ldots,X_{f(G_{k})})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(G_{1}X_{1},\ldots,G_{k}X_{1})\hbox to0.0pt{$\,=T^{\prime}X_{1}$.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{f(H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(c_{G_{1}},\ldots,c_{G_{k}})}

Then we obtain the
T
-coalgebra

\mathsf{Comp}(\bar{X},\bar{\xi})=\big(X_{1}\xrightarrow{c_{T}}TX_{f(T)}=TX_{1}\big),

and on morphisms put
\mathsf{Comp}(h_{1},\ldots,h_{n})=h_{1}
.
It is not difficult to prove that

\mathsf{Comp}(\mathsf{Pad}(X,\xi))=(X,\xi),

see
[
SP11
, Text after Lemma 4.11]
.

Example 8.4
.

Given a coalgebra

\xi\colon X\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}X\times\mathcal{D}(A\times X))
for
T
as in

8.3
,
\mathsf{Pad}(X,\xi)
is the following
\bar{T}
-coalgebra:

\begin{array}[]{l}(X,\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}X\times\mathcal{D}(A\times X),\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}X,\mathcal{D}(A\times X),A\times X)\xrightarrow{(\xi,\mathsf{id},\mathsf{id},\mathsf{id},\mathsf{id})}\\
\qquad(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}X\times\mathcal{D}(A\times X)),\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}X\times\mathcal{D}(A\times X),\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}X,\mathcal{D}(A\times X),A\times X)\end{array}

Given a
\bar{T}
-coalgebra

\bar{\xi}\colon\bar{X}=(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4},X_{5})\xrightarrow{(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4},\xi_{5})}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X_{2},X_{3}\times X_{4},\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}X_{1},\mathcal{D}X_{5},A\times X_{1})=\bar{T}\bar{X}

we obtain the following
T
-coalgebra
\mathsf{Comp}(\bar{X},\bar{\xi})
on
X_{1}
:

X_{1}\xrightarrow{\xi_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X_{2}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}\xi_{2}}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(X_{3}\times X_{4})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\xi_{3}\times\xi_{4})}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}X_{1}\times\mathcal{D}X_{5})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathsf{id}\times\mathcal{D}\xi_{5})}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}X_{1}\times\mathcal{D}(A\times X_{1})).

The above coalgebra
\mathsf{Pad}(X,\xi)
is no longer finite; e.g.
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(X)
is
infinite for nonempty
X
. However, one can find a finite

\bar{T}
-coalgebra that conforms to
\xi
by restricting e.g. the
sort
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(X)
to those elements of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(X)
that actually appear
in
\xi
.

Note that a functor
H\colon\mathsf{Set}^{k}\to\mathsf{Set}
is finitary if
and only if for every finite set
X
and every map

g\colon X\to H(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})
there exist finite subsets

m_{i}\colon Y_{i}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Y_{i}
such that
g
factorizes through

H(m_{1},\ldots,m_{k})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(Y_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,Y_{k}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(m_{1},\ldots,m_{k})}

(8.1)

In situations where
Y_{i}=G(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell})
for another finitary
functor
G\colon\mathsf{Set}^{\ell}\to\mathsf{Set}
, this process can be repeated for
each of the
m_{i}\colon Y_{i}^{\prime}\to G(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell})
. Formally:

Construction 12
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a set of finitary functors, let

T\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
be a functor composed from
\mathcal{H}
as
in
8.2
with flattening

\bar{T}\colon\mathsf{Set}^{n}\to\mathsf{Set}^{n}
(and a bijection
f
), and let

(X,\xi)
be a finite
T
-coalgebra. We construct a

\bar{T}
-coalgebra
\mathsf{Factor}(X,\xi)
by repeatedly applying the
above factorization technique. In this way we obtain finite sets

X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
, for every subterm
T^{\prime}
a map

m_{T^{\prime}}\colon X_{f(T^{\prime})}\to T^{\prime}X
, and for every non-leaf subterm

T^{\prime}=H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k})
of
T
a map

\bar{\xi}_{T^{\prime}}\colon X_{f(T^{\prime})}\to H(X_{f(G_{1})},\ldots,X_{f(G_{k})})
. More precisely, we start by putting

m_{T}:=\xi\colon X_{1}:=X\to TX
and then proceed recursively
down the syntax tree of
T
. For
T^{\prime}=(-)
we put

m_{(-)}=\mathsf{id}_{X}\colon X_{1}=X\to X
, and for a non-leaf
subterm
T^{\prime}=H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k})
we apply the above factorization to
(the already chosen)
m_{T^{\prime}}
, i.e. we choose finite subsets

m_{G_{i}}\colon X_{f(G_{i})}\hookrightarrow G_{i}X
,
i=1,\ldots,k
, and a
map
\bar{\xi}_{T^{\prime}}
such that the triangle below commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(G_{1}X,\ldots,G_{k}X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(X_{f(G_{1})},\ldots,X_{f(G_{n})}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{T^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\xi}_{T^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(m_{G_{1}},\ldots,m_{G_{n}})}

(8.2)

For
G_{i}\neq(-)
, we choose

m_{G_{i}}\colon X_{f(G_{i})}\hookrightarrow G_{i}(X)
to be a minimal
finite subset admitting such a factorization
\bar{\xi}_{T^{\prime}}
, and
for
G_{i}=(-)
we use

m_{(-)}=\mathsf{id}_{X}
as defined above. Note that we keep track of
duplicate subterms as in
8.2
.
The
i^{\text{th}}
component of the

\bar{T}
-coalgebra
\mathsf{Factor}(X,\xi)
is now defined using

f^{-1}(i)=H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k})
by

X_{i}=X_{f(H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}))}\xrightarrow{\bar{\xi}_{H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k})}}H(X_{f(G_{1})},\ldots,X_{f(G_{k})})=(\bar{T}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))_{i}.

Note that the sets
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are finite, and for
T^{\prime}\neq T
,

m_{T^{\prime}}
is injective.

Proposition 8.5
.

For every
T
-coalgebra
(X,\xi)
we have

(X,\xi)=\mathsf{Comp}(\mathsf{Factor}(X,\xi)).

Moreover,

\bar{m}=\big(\mathsf{Factor}(X,\xi)\xrightarrow{(\mathsf{id}_{X},m_{f^{-1}(2)},m_{f^{-1}(3)},\ldots,m_{f^{-1}(n)})}\mathsf{Pad}(X,\xi)\big)

is a
\bar{T}
-coalgebra morphism, in fact a subcoalgebra inclusion.

Proof 8.6
.

We first verify that
\bar{m}
is a
\bar{T}
-coalgebra morphism. We do this
component-wise and by case distinction.

In the first component, we have
f^{-1}(1)=T=H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k})
and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{in }\mathsf{Factor}(X,\xi):}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(X_{f(G_{1})},\ldots,X_{f(G_{k})})\mathrlap{\ =\bar{T}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{in }\mathsf{Pad}(X,\xi):}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(G_{1}(X),\ldots,G_{k}(X))\mathrlap{\ =\bar{T}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{m}_{1}=\mathsf{id}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\xi}_{H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k})}}

(
8.2
)

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(m_{G_{1}},\ldots,m_{G_{k}})=(\bar{T}(\bar{m}))_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi=m_{T}}

In all other components we have
f^{-1}(i)=T^{\prime}=H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k})
and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{in }\mathsf{Factor}(X,\xi):}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{f(H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(X_{f(G_{1})},\ldots,X_{f(G_{k})})\mathrlap{\ =\bar{T}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{in }\mathsf{Pad}(X,\xi):}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(G_{1}(X),\ldots,G_{k}(X)).\mathrlap{\ =\bar{T}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{m}_{i}=m_{f^{-1}(i)}=m_{T^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\xi}_{H(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k})}}

(
8.2
)

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(m_{G_{1}},\ldots,m_{G_{k}})=(\bar{T}(\bar{m}))_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}}

We know that
\mathsf{Comp}
is a functor and that

\mathsf{Comp}(\mathsf{Pad}(X,\xi))=(X,\xi)
. Then the definition of

\mathsf{Comp}
on morphisms yields

\mathsf{Comp}(\bar{m})=\bar{m}_{1}=\mathsf{id}_{X}
and therefore the desired identity.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Comp}(\mathsf{Factor}(X,\xi))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Comp}(\mathsf{Pad}(X,\xi))=(X,\xi).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Comp}(\bar{m})}

Remark 8.7
.

We do not need
\mathsf{Factor}
to be functorial. In fact, it is
functorial if every
H\colon\mathsf{Set}^{k}\to\mathsf{Set}
in
\mathcal{H}

preserves inverse images. However, some of our functors of interest,
e.g.
\mathds{R}^{(-)}
, do not preserve inverse images.

Example 8.8
.

Consider a finite coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to TX
for the functor

T=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(-)\times\mathcal{D}(A\times(-)))
from

8.3
. The above
12

yields a (multisorted)
\bar{T}
-coalgebra
\mathsf{Factor}(X,\xi)
with
finite carriers
(X,X_{2},X_{3},X_{4},X_{5})
, and structure maps

\begin{array}[]{r@{}l@{\qquad}r@{}l@{\qquad}r@{}l}\bar{\xi}_{T}&\colon X\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X_{2}&\bar{\xi}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(-)\times\mathcal{D}(A\times(-))}&\colon X_{2}\to X_{3}\times X_{4}&\bar{\xi}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(-)}&\colon X_{3}\to\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}X\\
\bar{\xi}_{\mathcal{D}(A\times(-))}&\colon X_{4}\to\mathcal{D}X_{5}&\bar{\xi}_{A\times(-)}&\colon X_{5}\to A\times X.\end{array}

For our purposes it is crucial that we may compute the
simple quotient of
\mathsf{Factor}(X,\xi)
and obtain from its first
component the simple quotient of
(X,\xi)
.

Proposition 8.9
.

If a
\bar{T}
-coalgebra
(\bar{X},\bar{\xi})
is simple, then so is
the
T
-coalgebra
\mathsf{Comp}(\bar{X},\bar{\xi})
.

Proof 8.10
.

Let
q\colon\mathsf{Comp}(\bar{X},\bar{\xi})\to(Y,\zeta)
; by

Section 2.2
, we need to show that
q
is monic. From
q
,
we obtain a
\bar{T}
-coalgebra morphism
q^{\prime}\colon(X,\xi)\to\mathsf{Pad}(Y,\zeta)

by adjoint transposition; that is,

q^{\prime}=\big((\bar{X},\bar{\xi})\xrightarrow{\eta_{(\bar{X},\bar{\xi})}=(\mathsf{id}_{X_{1}},m_{2},\ldots,m_{n})}\mathsf{Pad}(\mathsf{Comp}(\bar{X},\bar{\xi}))\xrightarrow{\mathsf{Pad}(q)=(q,q_{2},\ldots,q_{n})}\mathsf{Pad}(Y,\zeta)\big).

Since
(\bar{X},\bar{\xi})
is simple,
q^{\prime}
is monic in
\mathsf{Set}^{n}
; in
particular, the first component
q
of
q^{\prime}
is monic, as required.

Corollary 8.11
.

If
q\colon\mathsf{Factor}(X,\xi)\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{{\ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}{{
{
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\ignorespaces\lxSVG@buttcap\ignorespaces{}
{}
{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.72014pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.96007pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}(Y,\zeta)
represents the
simple quotient of the
\bar{T}
-coalgebra
\mathsf{Factor}(X,\xi)
, then

\mathsf{Comp}(q)\colon(X,\xi)\to\mathsf{Comp}(Y,\zeta)
represents the simple
quotient of
(X,\xi)
.

In short, the problem of minimizing single-sorted coalgebras
for functors composed in some way from functors
H
reduces to
minimizing multi-sorted coalgebras for the components
H
. In the
next subsection, we will, in turn, reduce the latter problem to
minimizing single-sorted coalgebras, using however coproducts of the
components
H
in lieu of
\mathsf{Comp}
. The benefit of this seemingly
roundabout procedure is that refinement interfaces, which fail to
combine along functor composition, do propagate along coproducts of
functors as we show in Subsection
8.3
.

8.2.
De-sorting multisorted coalgebras

We fix a number
n
of sorts, and consider coalgebras over
\mathcal{C}^{n}
. As
before, we assume that
\mathcal{C}
, and hence also
\mathcal{C}^{n}
, fulfils

Section 2.1
. We assume moreover that
H\colon\mathcal{C}^{n}\to\mathcal{C}^{n}
is a
mono-preserving functor modelling the transition type of multisorted
coalgebras. We now show that under two additional assumptions on
\mathcal{C}
,
one can equivalently transform
H
-coalgebras into single-sorted
coalgebras, i.e. coalgebras on
\mathcal{C}
, formed by taking the coproduct
of the carriers, a process we refer to as

de-sorting
. Specifically, we need
\mathcal{C}
to have finite
coproducts (implying finite cocompleteness in combination with

Section 2.1
) and to be
extensive

[
CLW93
]
. We begin by
taking a closer look at these additional assumptions in the setting of

\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{n}
.

Notation 13
.

We have the usual diagonal functor

\Delta\colon\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{n}\qquad\Delta(X)=(X,\ldots,X).

This functor has a left adjoint given by taking coproducts
(e.g.
[
Awo10
, p. 225]
), which we denote by

\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}\colon\mathcal{C}^{n}\to\mathcal{C}\qquad\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})=X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n}.

The unit
\eta_{X}\colon X\to\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}X
of the adjunction
consists of the coproduct injections, and the adjoint transpose of a

\mathcal{C}^{n}
-morphism
f\colon X\to\Delta Y
, denoted

[f]\colon\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}X\to Y
, arises by cotupling.

{defiC}

[
[
CLW93
]
]
A category
\mathcal{C}
with finite coproducts is called
extensive
if
the canonical functor
\mathcal{C}/Y_{1}\times\mathcal{C}/Y_{2}\to\mathcal{C}/{Y_{1}+Y_{2}}
is an
equivalence of categories for every pair
Y_{1},Y_{2}
of objects.

Remark 8.12
.

(1)

This compact definition can be equivalently rephrased as
follows
[
CLW93
, Proposition 2.2]
:
\mathcal{C}
has pullbacks along
coproduct injections and a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}+Y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{inl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{inr}}

(8.3)

comprises two pullback squares if and only if the top row is a
coproduct diagram.

(2)

It easy to see that coproduct
injections in an extensive category
\mathcal{C}
are monomorphic, hence

\eta_{X}\colon X\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{{\ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{{
{{{\ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\ignorespaces\lxSVG@buttcap\ignorespaces{}
{}
{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.82 0 L 16.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.19998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L -2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.96007pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}X
is a
monomorphism in
\mathcal{C}^{n}
.

Example 8.13
.

Categories with
\mathsf{Set}
-like coproducts are extensive, in particular
\mathsf{Set}

itself, the category of partially ordered sets and monotone maps, and the
category of nominal sets and equivariant maps. Presheaf categories
are extensive, and more generally, so is every Grothendieck topos.

We shall make use of the following equivalent description of extensivity:

Proposition 8.14
.

A category
\mathcal{C}
is extensive if and only if for
every
n\geq 0
we have that a commutative square in
\mathcal{C}^{n}
as below
is a pullback iff
[f]\colon\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}X^{\prime}\to X
is
an isomorphism.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{Y}}

Proof 8.15
.

For
n=0
and
n=1
the above equivalence always holds using that,
for
n=1
,
\eta_{Y}=\mathsf{id}_{Y}
. We consider the case
n=2
. The above
diagram then reads:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{1}^{\prime},X_{2}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y_{1},Y_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y_{1}+Y_{2},Y_{1}+Y_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1},f_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h,h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{inl},\mathsf{inr})}

This is a pullback in
\mathcal{C}^{2}
iff each of its components is a
pullback in
\mathcal{C}
, i.e. the two squares in (
8.3
) are
pullbacks. The adjoint transpose
[f]
is the morphism
[f_{1},f_{2}]\colon X_{1}^{\prime}+X_{2}^{\prime}\to X
, which is an isormorphism if and only if
the top row in (
8.3
) is a coproduct diagram. Hence, the
equivalence in the statement of the proposition for
n=2
is
equivalent to extensivity. We are done since for
n>2

that equivalence clearly follows from that for
n=2
.

Our goal in this section is to relate
H
-coalgebras (in

\mathcal{C}^{n}
) with
\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}H\Delta
-coalgebras (in
\mathcal{C}
). This is
via two observations:

(1)

The obvious functor from
H
-coalgebras to

H\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}
-coalgebras given by

(X\xrightarrow{x}HX)\mapsto(X\xrightarrow{x}HX\xrightarrow{H\eta_{X}}H\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}X)

preserves and reflects (simple) quotients
(
8.16
).

(2)

The categories of
\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}H\Delta
-coalgebras and

H\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}
-coalgebras are equivalent
(
8.17
).

Since
H
preserves monomorphisms we have a natural transformation
H\eta\colon H\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{{\ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{{
{{{\ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\ignorespaces\lxSVG@buttcap\ignorespaces{}
{}
{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.82 0 L 16.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.19998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L -2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{{{\ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.96007pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.55 L 2.55 0 L 0 -2.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}H\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}
with monic components. The first translation
thus follows from
Section 2.2
:

Corollary 8.16
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is extensive, then a
H
-coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to HX
and
its induced
H\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}
-coalgebra

X\xrightarrow{\xi}HX\xrightarrow{H\eta_{X}}H\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}X

have the same quotients and, hence, the same simple quotient.

Lemma 8.17
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is extensive, then the lifting
\bar{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}}
of the
coproduct functor
\scriptstyle\coprod

\bar{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}}\colon\mathsf{Coalg}(H\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$})\to\mathsf{Coalg}(\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}H\Delta),\qquad\bar{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}}(X\xrightarrow{\xi}H\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}X)=(\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}X\xrightarrow{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}\xi}\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}H\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}X)
