is an equivalence of categories.

Proof 8.18
.

We have to show that
\bar{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}}
is full, faithful and
isomorphism-dense
[
AHS90
]
. Faithfulness is immediate from
the fact that already
\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}\colon\mathcal{C}^{n}\to\mathcal{C}
is faithful, since
coproduct injections are monic
(
8.12
(2)
). To see
that
\bar{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}}
is full, let

h\colon\bar{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}}(X,\xi)\to\bar{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}}(Y,\zeta)

be a
\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}H\Delta
-coalgebra morphism. By naturality
of
\eta
, we then have a commuting diagram

\hbox to324.61pt{\vbox to82.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 162.30148pt\lower-41.31944pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-162.30148pt}{-41.31944pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\text{in }\mathcal{C}^{n}.

We have the indicated pullback by extensivity (since
[\eta_{Y}]=\mathsf{id}
), and thus
obtain
h^{\prime}\colon X\to Y
such that
\zeta\cdot h^{\prime}=H\Delta h\cdot\xi
and

\eta_{Y}\cdot h^{\prime}=\Delta h\cdot\eta_{X}
. Using naturality of
\eta

the latter equality yields

\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}h^{\prime}\cdot\eta_{X}=\eta_{Y}\cdot h^{\prime}=\Delta h\cdot\eta_{X},

which implies that
\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}h^{\prime}=h
since
\eta_{X}
is
a universal morphism. Now the first equality above states that

h^{\prime}\colon(X,\xi)\to(Y,\zeta)
is an

H\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}
-coalgebra morphism.

It remains to show that
\bar{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}}
is
isomorphism-dense. So let
(X,\xi)
be a

\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}H\Delta
-coalgebra. Form the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\Delta X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}H\Delta X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{H\Delta X}}

(8.4)

in
\mathcal{C}^{n}
. Since
\mathcal{C}
is extensive,

[x]\colon\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}X^{\prime}\to X
is an isomorphism; we thus have
an
H\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}
-coalgebra

X^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\xi^{\prime}}H\Delta X\xrightarrow{H\Delta[x]^{-1}}H\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}X^{\prime}.

Applying the adjunction to the square (
8.4
) shows

\xi\cdot[x]=\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}\xi^{\prime}
. It follows that the
isomorphism
[x]
is a coalgebra morphism, and hence an isomorphism
in
\mathsf{Coalg}(\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}H\Delta)
, from

\bar{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}}(X^{\prime},H\Delta[x]^{-1}\cdot\xi^{\prime})
to
(X,\xi)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}H\Delta X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}H\Delta\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}H\Delta X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[x]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\coprod$}\xi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\coprod$}H\Delta[x]^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\coprod$}H\Delta[x]}

So the task of computing the simple quotient of a
multisorted coalgebra is reduced again to the same problem on ordinary
coalgebras in
\mathsf{Set}
. Thus, it remains to check that the arising
functor
\Delta H\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}
indeed fulfils

7
.

8.3.
Coproducts of refinement
interfaces

We have already seen that zippable functors are closed under
coproducts (
5.5
). We proceed to show that we can
also combine refinement interface along coproducts. Let functors

H_{i}\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
,
1\leq i\leq n
have refinement interfaces
with labels
A_{i}
and weights
W_{i}
, and associated functions

\flat_{i},\mathsf{init}_{i},w_{i},\mathsf{update}_{i}
. We construct a refinement
interface for the coproduct
H={\textstyle\coprod}H_{i}
, with labels

A={\textstyle\coprod}A_{i}
and weights
W={\textstyle\coprod}W_{i}
, as follows.
First define the following helper function, which restricts a multiset
of labels to a given sort
i
:

\textstyle\mathsf{filter}_{i}\colon\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(\textstyle\coprod_{j=1}^{n}A_{j})\to\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}(A_{i}),\qquad\mathsf{filter}_{i}(f)(a)=f(\mathsf{in}_{i}(a)).

(Note that this differs from the filter function
\mathsf{fil}_{S}
in
(
6.1
), which filters for a subset of states

S\subseteq X
.) Then we implement the refinement interface for
H

component-wise as follows (writing
I=\{1,\dots,n\}
):

Proposition 8.19
.

The data
W
,
A
,
\flat
,
\mathsf{init}
,
w
, and
\mathsf{update}
as
constructed above form a refinement interface for

H={\textstyle\coprod}H_{i}
, and if the interfaces of the
H_{i}
fulfil

7
, then so does the one of
H
.

Proof 8.20
.

For
i=1,\ldots,n
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textstyle\coprod}H_{j}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textstyle\coprod}H_{j}1\times\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}{\textstyle\coprod}A_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}1\times\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{j}\big(H_{j}1\times\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}\coprod_{k}A_{k}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textstyle\coprod}(H_{j}1\times\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}A_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textstyle\coprod}W_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{r}\langle H!,\\[-5.69054pt]
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{1}\cdot\flat\rangle\end{array}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{in}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{r}\langle H_{i}!,\\[-5.69054pt]
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}\pi_{1}\cdot\flat_{i}\rangle\end{array}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{i}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{in}_{i}(t),a)\mapsto\mathsf{in}_{i}(t,a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{init}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{init}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{in}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{in}_{i}\times\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathsf{in}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{in}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod(\mathsf{id}\times\mathsf{filter}_{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod\mathsf{init}_{j}}

Since the
\mathsf{in}_{i}\colon H_{i}Y\to{\textstyle\coprod}H_{i}Y
are jointly epic, the
commutativity shows the axiom for
\mathsf{init}
in (
6.1
).
For
S\subseteq C\subseteq Y
we have the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{{\textstyle\coprod}}H_{i}Y}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\flat,w(C)\rangle}

Using again that the
\mathsf{in}_{i}\colon H_{i}Y\to{\textstyle\coprod}H_{i}Y
are

the axiom for
\mathsf{update}
in (
6.1
). If for
every

, the interface of
H_{i}
fulfils the time constraints from

, then so does the interface for
H
: both

\mathsf{init}
and
\mathsf{update}
preprocess the parameters in linear

\mathsf{filter}
), before calling the
\mathsf{init}_{i}
and

\mathsf{update}_{i}
of the corresponding
H_{i}
. We order
H3

lexicographically, i.e.
\mathsf{in}_{i}(x)<\mathsf{in}_{j}(y)
iff either
i<j
or

i=j
and
x<y
in
H_{i}3
. Comparison in constant time is then
clearly inherited.

).
Webuildourexamplesfromthefunctors

\mathcal{D},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}},A\times(-)\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}\qquad\times,+\colon\mathsf{Set}^{2}\to\mathsf{Set},

.

Example 8.21
.

(1)

Labelled transition systems
with an infinite set
A
of
labels. Here we decompose the coalgebraic type functor

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times(-))
into
H_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
and
H_{2}=A\times(-)
. We
transform a coalgebra
X\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times X)
(with
m
edges) into
a multisorted
(H_{1},H_{2})
-coalgebra; the new sort
Y
then contains
one element per edge. By de-sorting, we finally obtain a
single-sorted coalgebra for
\bar{H}=H_{1}+H_{2}
with
n+m
states
and
m
edges, leading to a complexity of

\mathcal{O}((n+m)\cdot\log(n+m))
. If
m\geq n
we thus obtain a run-time
in
\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\log m)
, like in
[
DPP04
]
but slower
than Valmari’s
\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\log n)

[
Val09
]
.

For fixed finite
A
, the running time of our algorithm is in

\mathcal{O}((m+n)\log n)
. Indeed, by finiteness of
A
, we have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times(-))\cong\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(-)^{A}
. Then a coalgebra

X\xrightarrow{\xi}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times X)\cong\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(X)^{A}
with
n=|X|

states and
m=\sum_{x\in X}|\xi(x)|
edges is transformed into a
two-sorted coalgebra

\eta_{X}\colon X\to(A\times X)^{A},\;x\mapsto(a\mapsto(a,x));\qquad\bar{\xi}\colon A\times X\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(X),\;(a,x)\mapsto\xi(x)(a).

The arising de-sorted system on
X+A\times X
has
n+|A|\cdot n

states and
|A|\cdot n+m
edges, so the simple quotient is found
in
\mathcal{O}((m+n)\cdot\log n)
like in the single-sorted approach
(
7.21
).

(1)

As mentioned already, Hopcroft’s classical automata
minimization
[
Hop71
]
is obtained by instantiating our
approach to
HX=2\times X^{A}
, with running time
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\log n)
for
fixed alphabet
A
. For non-fixed
A
the best known complexity is
in
\mathcal{O}(|A|\cdot n\cdot\log n)

[
Gri73
,
Knu01
]
. To
obtain the alphabet as part of the input to our algorithm, we
consider DFAs as labelled transition systems encoding the letters of
the input alphabet as natural numbers. More precisely, given a
finite input alphabet
A
, we choose
some injective encoding map
c\colon A\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

and we form the natural
transformation with the components

m_{X}\colon 2\times X^{A}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to12.4pt{\vbox to4.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.19998pt\lower-2.03995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}2\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathds{N}\times X)\qquad\text{with}\qquad m_{X}(b,f)=\big(b,\{(c(a),f(a))\mid a\in A\}\big).

Since
m_{X}
is clearly monomorphic, we apply

Section 2.2
to see that minimization of a DFA
\xi\colon X\to 2\times X^{A}
is reduced to minimizing the coalgebra

X\xrightarrow{\xi}2\times X^{A}\xrightarrow{m_{X}}2\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathds{N}\times X).

Further, we decompose the type
functor into
H_{1}=2\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}
and
H_{2}=\mathds{N}\times(-)
. An
automaton
\xi
for a finite input alphabet
A

is then represented by the two-sorted system

\xi\colon X\to 2\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times X)\qquad c\times X\colon A\times X\to\mathds{N}\times X

With
|X|=n
, this system has
n+|A|\cdot n
states and

|A|\cdot n+|A|\cdot n
edges. Thus, our algorithm runs in time

\mathcal{O}((|A|\cdot n)\cdot\log(|A|\cdot n))=\mathcal{O}(|A|\cdot n\cdot\log n+|A|\cdot n\cdot\log|A|),

i.e. as fast as the above-mentioned best known algorithms except on
automata with more alphabet letters than states.

(2)

Coalgebras for the functor

HX=\mathcal{D}X+\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times X)
are
alternating
systems

[
Han94
]
. The functor
H
is
flattened to the multi-sorted functor

\bar{H}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4})=(X_{2}+X_{3},\mathcal{D}X_{1},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X_{4},A\times X_{1})

on
\mathsf{Set}^{4}
, which is then de-sorted to obtain the
\mathsf{Set}
-functor

\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}\bar{H}\Delta X=(X+X)+\mathcal{D}X+\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}X+A\times X

which has a refinement interface as given by

8.19
. Given an
H
-coalgebra with
n
states
and
m_{d}
edges of type
\mathcal{D}
and
m_{p}
edges of type

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times(-))
, the induced

\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\coprod$}\bar{H}\Delta
-coalgebra has
n+m_{p}
states and

n+m_{d}+m_{p}+m_{p}
edges, and is minimized under bisimilarity in time

\mathcal{O}((n+m_{d}+m_{p})\cdot\log(n+m_{p})).

Other probabilistic system types
[
BSdV03
]
are handled
similarly, where one only needs to take care of estimating the
number of states in the intermediate sorts as in the treatment
above. We discuss two further examples explicitly, simple and
general Segala systems.

(3)

For a simple Segala system considered as a coalgebra
\xi\colon X\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(A\times\mathcal{D}X)
there are partition refinement algorithms by Baier,
Engelen, Majster-Cederbaum
[
BEM00
]
and by Groote, Verduzco, and de
Vink
[
GVdV18
]
. For the complexity analysis, define the number of
states and edges respectively as

\textstyle n=|X|,\qquad m_{p}=\sum_{x\in X}|\xi(x)|.

The arising multi-sorted coalgebra consists of maps

p\colon X\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}Y\qquad a\colon Y\to A\times Z\qquad d\colon Z\to\mathcal{D}X.

In the coalgebra
\xi
there is one distribution per
non-deterministic edge, hence
|Y|=m_{p}=|Z|
. The
non-deterministic map
p
has
m_{p}
edges by construction, and the
deterministic map
a
has
|Y|=m_{p}
edges. Let
m_{d}
denote the
number of edges needed to encode
d
; then
m_{d}\leq n\cdot m_{p}
. We
thus have
n+2\cdot m_{p}
states and
2\cdot m_{p}+m_{d}
edges, so
our algorithm runs in time
\mathcal{O}((n+m_{p}+m_{d})\cdot\log(n+m_{p}))
.
For every
z\in Z
,
d(z)
is a non-empty distribution, and so under
the assumption that there is at least one non-deterministic edge per
state
x\in X
, we have
m_{d}\geq m_{p}\geq n
, simplifying the
complexity to
\mathcal{O}(m_{d}\cdot\log m_{p})
. In independent work, Groote
et al.
[
GVdV18
]
consider
Z
as part of the input, and
design and implement an algorithm of time complexity

\mathcal{O}((m_{p}+m_{d})\cdot\log|Z|+m_{d}\cdot\log n)
, which simplifies
to the same complexity
\mathcal{O}(m_{d}\cdot\log m_{p})
for
|Z|=m_{p}
and

m_{d}\geq m_{p}\geq n
. This is more fine-grained than the complexity

\mathcal{O}((n\cdot m_{p})\cdot\log(n+m_{p}))=\mathcal{O}((n\cdot m_{p})\cdot\log(n\cdot m_{p}))

of
[
BEM00
]
, and indeed leads to a faster run-time in the
(presumably wide-spread) case that probabilistic transitions are
sparse, i.e. if
m_{d}
is substantially below
n\cdot m_{p}
.

(4)

For a general Segala system

\xi\colon X\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{D}(A\times X))
one has a similar
factorization:

p\colon X\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}Y\qquad d\colon Y\to\mathcal{D}Z\qquad z\colon Z\to A\times X

So for
n=|X|
states,
m_{p}
non-deterministic edges, and
m_{d}
probabilistic
edges, the multisorted system has
n+|Y|+|Z|=n+m_{p}+m_{d}
states and

m_{p}+m_{d}+m_{d}
edges. Since
d(y)
is non-empty for all
y\in Y
,
m_{d}\geq m_{p}
, and so the generic partition refinement has a run-time of

\mathcal{O}(m_{d}\cdot\log(n+m_{d}))
.

Summingupthelastthreeexamples
,
onsimpleSegalasystemsweobtainfasterrun
-
timethanthebestpreviousalgorithm

[
BEM00
]
(
withsimilarresultsobtainedindependentlybyGrooteetal
.
[
GVdV18
]
),
andweobtain
,
toourbestknowledge
,
thefirstsimilarlyefficientpartitionrefinementalgorithmsforalternatingsystems
(
Example

8.21
(2)
)
andgeneralSegalasystems
(
Example

8.21
(4)
).

9.
Conclusions and Further Work

We have presented a generic algorithm that quotients coalgebras by
behavioural equivalence. We have started from a category-theoretic
procedure that works for every mono-preserving functor on a category
with image factorizations, and have then developed an improved
algorithm for
zippable
endofunctors on
\mathsf{Set}
. Provided the
given type functor can be equipped with an efficient implementation of
a
refinement interface
, we have finally arrived at a concrete
procedure that runs in time
\mathcal{O}((m+n)\log n)
where
m
is the number
of edges and
n
the number of nodes in a graph-based representation
of the input coalgebra. We have shown that this instantiates to (minor
variants of) several known efficient partition refinement algorithms:
the classical Hopcroft algorithm
[
Hop71
]
for minimization of
DFAs, the Paige-Tarjan algorithm for unlabelled transition
systems
[
PT87
]
, Valmari and Franceschinis’s lumping
algorithm for weighted transition systems
[
VF10
]
, and the
1-dimensional Weisfeiler Lehman
Algorithm
[
CFI92
,
Wei76
,
SSvL+11
]
. Moreover,
we have presented a generic method to apply the algorithm to mixed
system types. As an instance, we obtain an algorithm for simple Segala
systems that allows for a more fine-grained analysis of asymptotic
run-time than previous algorithms
[
BEM00
]
, and matches the
run-time of a recent algorithm described independently by Groote
et al.
[
GVdV18
]
.

Further instances can be covered by relaxing the time complexity
assumptions on the refinement interfaces
[
DMSW19
]
, which allows
covering monoid-valued functors
M^{(-)}
also for non-cancellative
monoids
M
; by our compositionality methods, we obtain in particular
efficient partition refinement algorithm for
M
-weighted tree
automata, which improves the run-time of a previous algorithm by
Högberg, Maletti, and May
[
HMM07
]
.

In further work
[
DMSW19
]
, we describe and evaluate a generic
implementation
\mathsf{CoPaR}
of the generic algorithm. The implementation
supports unrestricted combination of functors with refinement
interfaces (cf.
Section 8
) and implements all the
functors from
Section 7
.

It remains open whether our approach can be extended to, e.g. the monotone
neighbourhood functor, which is not itself zippable (see
5.11
) and
also does not have an obvious factorization into zippable functors. We do expect
that our algorithm applies beyond weighted systems. For example, it should be
relatively straightforward to extend our algorithm to nominal systems,
i.e. coalgebras for functors on the category of nominal sets and equivariant
maps. Of course, precise complexity bounds will then depend on the
representation of nominal sets.
